#288. Vance says he was assured Jensen Huang was not under DOJ investigation

Vice President JD Vance said Wednesday that Attorney General Todd Blanche told him Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang was not under investigation.

"I was told by the Department of Justice and multiple officials in our national security apparatus that he was under no investigation," Vance told reporters in Ohio on Wednesday after being asked what assurances he had gotten that Huang was not under investigation. "Not even that he was about to be prosecuted; that he's under no investigation."

Vance spoke two days after President Donald Trump told CNBC in an interview that an investigation had been done.

Trump was asked what Blanche had told him about potential criminal liability for Huang and the question of whether Nvidia chips had ended up in China in violation of U.S. export laws.

"They did a pretty good investigation, and they felt that he, himself, was exonerated," Trump said Monday.

Reuters reported Wednesday that the Department of Justice did not conduct a formal investigation into Huang before Trump pushed for his reinstatement as CEO.

Blanche had concluded there was no evidence Huang had broken any laws based on investigations into Nvidia clients Super Micro Computer and Megaspeed, according to the report.

The DOJ did not immediately respond to a request for comment on the Reuters report. And the White House didn't immediately respond to a request for comment on Vance saying Huang was under no investigation when Trump had said there was one. Nvidia didn't immediately respond to a request for comment about Vance's remarks.

Blanche on Tuesday dodged questions about whether there had been an investigation.

"What the president said is that he talked to a lot of people going into the decision that he made to encourage the board to reinstate him," Blanche said after being asked whether the DOJ had investigated Huang and, if so, what were the results of the probe.

Huang was reinstated as CEO of Nvidia on Monday, after the chipmaker's board of directors said it had received "input from senior levels of the United States government" that Huang was not a national security risk. Trump took credit for Huang's reinstatement in the interview with CNBC shortly after Nvidia's announcement.

Huang resigned following The New York Times reporting multiple affairs, including one with a Chinese businesswoman who is alleged to be a spy for Beijing and who was an executive for the Singapore-based cloud provider Megaspeed.

The reporting added fuel to allegations that Nvidia should have known its artificial intelligence chips were being diverted to China and being used by Chinese companies, violating U.S. export laws.

"The product they create is a very important part of us staying ahead of the SI arms race with China. We really do care about ensuring those chips do not fall into the wrong hands," Vance said, using the Trump administration's preferred acronym for AI, which the president calls "super intelligence."

"We actually see this is an opportunity for Nvidia to focus on complying with American export controls, and we think Jensen Huang is going to take that very seriously," Vance continued.

"We received the assurances that he was not under any significant — he was under no investigation — and the accusations against him were based on anonymous reporting, not on actual evidence," Vance said.

On Tuesday, Trump left the door open to pardon Huang in an interview with the New York Post, but Vance said on Wednesday that the CEO doesn't need one.

"I don't think he needs a pardon at all," Vance said. "I haven't seen any evidence again that he committed a crime. And if there is evidence that he committed a crime, then that's up to relevant authorities to actually bring a prosecution against him."

Huang is scheduled to appear for a closed-door interview with House committees about his dealings with Megaspeed on Tuesday. At the same time, Nvidia will hold its annual conference where it showcases new technology in Washington.

— Kevin Breuninger contributed to this report.

#287. Gap names new Athleta CEO, says Old Navy turnaround is seeing signs of progress

Gap Inc. on Thursday announced Ashley Kechter will be the next president and CEO of its Athleta brand, effective Dec. 14.

The retailer also said Old Navy's turnaround is showing signs of progress, sending shares up more than 11% Friday.

Kechter comes to Gap from activewear retailer Vuori, where she was global president and oversaw the brand's global business strategy, marketing and financials. She was previously global brand president of Fabletics and began her career at Gap before that.

"I'm excited to return to Gap Inc. and partner with [Gap CEO Richard Dickson] and the team to strengthen our connection with customers and shape the brand's next chapter," Kechter said in a statement.

The announcement comes after Lululemon poached former Athleta CEO Maggie Gauger to be its chief product officer last month.

"Clearly, we're disappointed with Athleta's performance, and we recognize there's a lot of work ahead," Dickson told CNBC. "We had been in rebuild mode, but now we're really in turnaround mode."

Dickson said the company is focused on getting back to its fundamentals: fabrications, feature, form, function and fit. Dickson added that Kechter will be conducting a "comprehensive review" of the brand to reposition Athleta for future success.

While the execution of its strategy has thus far "fallen short," Dickson said this new approach will allow Athleta to fully realize its potential.

Gap also reported mixed fiscal third-quarter earnings on Thursday and posted flat overall comparable sales. Wall Street had been expecting a comparable sales increase of 1.3%, according to StreetAccount.

Net sales grew 2%, though demand softened in October and early November due to warmer weather, the company said. Store sales increased 1%, while online sales increased 3%.

Gap also said it's recorded a $420 million adjustment to cost of goods sold and a $21 million adjustment to interest income from tariff refunds so far this year.

The company lowered its full-year net sales outlook to be "roughly flat," down from previous guidance of up between 1% and 1.5%. Gap is also now expecting adjusted earnings per share of between $2.29 and $2.34, down from an earlier expectation of between $2.35 and $2.45 per share.

Dickson said in an interview that much of the lowered guidance was due to warmer weather in the first two weeks of the quarter, but he said Gap is expecting "meaningful acceleration" in shopping ahead of the peak holiday season.

"We're monitoring the trends, and we're going to remain agile as we respond to the changing business trends," Dickson said.

Here's how the company performed in its fiscal third quarter compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

For the quarter, Gap reported net income of $311 million, or 86 cents per share, compared with $236 million, or 62 cents per share, the year prior. Sales rose slightly from $3.94 billion the year prior.

At Athleta, net sales were down 14% compared with last year, coming in at $220 million. Comparable sales for the banner sank 15%, which the company attributed to "limited traction" from efforts to modernize its merchandise and marketing. `

The company's namesake brand saw 9% comparable sales growth for the quarter, with net sales of $1.1 billion, marking an 11% increase compared with last year. Dickson said it was the brand's 12th consecutive quarter of positive comparable sales.

"Gap is really firing on all cylinders, and the consistency of the brand is really giving us confidence in the brand's ability to continue its momentum," Dickson said.

The Old Navy banner saw flat comparable sales for the quarter and net sales of $2.3 billion, up 1% compared with last year. It's the first earnings report since the brand, which has been experiencing sluggish sales, named Michael Francis as its new CEO last quarter. Francis began his role at Old Navy on Nov. 2.

Banana Republic saw its net sales sink 3% compared with last year, which the company attributed to delayed demand for cold-weather categories and a women's fall assortment that fell flat. Wall Street had been expecting growth of 1.5%, according to StreetAccount.

Dickson said the brand has since introduced new products and seen demand "rebound" as cooler weather has set in across the country.

"We've been encouraged by the early customer response, and we should see sequential improvement in the fourth quarter," he said. "There's some work to do here, but with peak selling still ahead, I'm confident that the team is focused on the right areas and [will] ultimately position Banana well for growth."

#277. U.S. proposes new tariffs on 14 trading partners, including China and the EU

The U.S. is preparing new tariffs on 14 economies, including China, the EU, Japan and India, following investigations into what the Trump administration says are excess manufacturing practices that disadvantage U.S. commerce.

The administration launched those and other trade investigations in March, weeks after the Supreme Court unraveled a major piece of President Donald Trump's tariff-heavy economic policy by striking down his "liberation day" duties. Trump has since been accused by critics of his tariffs of trying to revive the canceled duties using other legal authorities.

The latest action targets foreign economies that create, sustain or contribute to structural excess capacity and production in manufacturing sectors that the U.S. finds unreasonable and burdensome or restrictive to its commerce, a senior U.S. official told reporters Tuesday evening.

The new tariffs are "targeted," not blanket rates, and will vary for each economy, and many will not exceed the rates established in previously reached trade deals, said the official, who spoke on condition of anonymity to discuss the forthcoming duties.

The rates, which mostly range from 15% to 20%, are "not really additive" and generally will not stack on top of existing duties, U.S. Trade Representative Jamieson Greer told CNBC's "Squawk Box" Wednesday morning.

"In effect, it'll look more like a top up," Greer said. "When it all nets out, it really will just top up a few percentage points."

An exception is China, which faces a 20% rate under the new tariff policy, "so it will go up some," Greer said. But at the same time, both countries are moving to remove tariffs on some of each other's goods in what Greer called "stability-generating activity through this action."

The senior U.S. official said Tuesday that the 20% rate will come on top of the so-called forced technology transfer-related tariffs of roughly 25%, which were implemented during Trump's first presidential term. U.S. tariffs on Chinese goods will therefore reach about 45%, give or take for different goods, the official said.

Average U.S. tariffs rates on Chinese goods stood at about 36.5% as of July, according to the Congressional Research Service.

Greer also said on CNBC that the new policy likely will not take effect for months.

"We've talked to our trading partners about this. They know what we're proposing," he said. "I think a lot of this is baked in, and I don't think any of these would actually take effect earlier than a couple months from now."

The administration says the tariffs target what it calls "overcapacity" — excess industrial production, often supported by government subsidies, that allows cheaper goods to flood global markets and undercut U.S. producers.

China has disputed the allegations. "The so-called 'overcapacity' does not really exist," China's foreign ministry said Wednesday before the official reports were released by the Office of the U.S. Trade Representative, according to Reuters.

The overcapacity levies are being imposed under Section 301 of the Trade Act of 1974. The Trump administration invoked the same statute to launch investigations into 60 economies over alleged forced-labor issues.

Those investigations, which also began in March, led to broad tariffs of 10% to 12.5% on goods from dozens of countries covering 99.4% of U.S. imports.

The Section 301 actions came weeks after the Supreme Court struck down the near-global tariffs that Trump had enacted under the International Emergency Economic Powers Act.

Shortly after the court's ruling, Trump invoked Section 122 of the Trade Act of 1974 to impose a 10% global tariff. That tariff expired after 150 days, and was immediately replaced with the forced-labor-related duties.

An ongoing lawsuit accuses the Trump administration of citing forced-labor issues as a "pretext" in pursuit of reviving Trump's worldwide tariff regime.

#269. A24 is having a great year at the box office. That could give HBO Max a streaming edge

Independent movie studio A24 has had a banner year at the box office, and HBO Max is set up to benefit from its success.

A24 is behind some of this year's buzziest films, including horror hit "Backrooms," dark comedy "The Drama" starring Zendaya and Robert Pattinson, and the Elizabeth Holmes documentary "You Can See Everything," co-directed by Nathan Fielder.

The studio has generated more than $526 million at the domestic box office this year as of Nov. 15, according to data from Rentrak. That's already roughly $290 million, or more than 50%, above its haul for all of 2025.

"They have an identity all their own, and just the mere mention of A24, the recognition that a movie is from A24, gives it instant credibility and provides a must-see factor, no matter what the subject matter," Paul Dergarabedian, head of marketplace trends at Rentrak, told CNBC.

Skydance co-CEO and Chairman David Ellison cited movie releases from A24 as part of the company's "unmatched portfolio" during its most recent earnings call.

A24 films released this year broke records at the box office, with "Backrooms" becoming the studio's biggest domestic opening to date and "You Can See Everything" notching the biggest documentary opening since "Fahrenheit 9/11" in 2004.

That success has already begun to translate to HBO Max's platform.

When "Backrooms" began streaming on HBO Max, it was the most streamed title on the platform for two weeks, according to Nielsen data. The film was also the fourth overall most streamed title the week it premiered, racking up 226 million minutes of viewing time.

After more than a year of competing bids and regulatory hurdles, Skydance now oversees two of the world's largest streaming platforms: HBO Max and Paramount+.

The company plans to eventually merge the streaming services into a single platform. It will take the first step in that effort by offering a value bundle in early 2027.

The combination of the platforms "will drive stronger engagement and higher lifetime value," co-CEO Ynon Kreiz said on the company's earnings call earlier this month.

Paramount Skydance's direct-to-consumer segment, which includes Paramount+ and its free, ad-supported channel, Pluto TV, generated more than $2 billion in revenue during the third quarter, representing more than 35% of total revenue for the period. That segment revenue marked an 11% year-over-year increase, with revenue from Paramount+ up 21%.

The earnings do not reflect the Warner Bros. Discovery acquisition, since that deal closed after the close of the quarter.

Revenue growth in streaming will be important as it faces the daunting task of managing nearly $80 billion of debt.

The company has said it expects to realize $6 billion in cost savings over the next three years and has put a particular focus on consolidating its operations and streamlining its marketing spend.

#261. Taking on Starlink: Europe’s largest satellite factory opens in push to rival Musk

The largest satellite factory in Europe was inaugurated in Belgium this week, boosting the continent's efforts to challenge Elon Musk's Starlink and reduce reliance on U.S. space infrastructure.

Privately held Belgian satellite manufacturer Aerospacelab secured a 2.4-billion-euro ($2.6 billion) contract in September to manufacture 264 of the 348 satellites planned for IRIS², the European Union's answer to SpaceX's Starlink business.

Aerospacelab said this will "form the backbone of Europe's sovereign, secure, and resilient connectivity infrastructure by 2030." It will be named Ectro from Nov. 30.

Its new oval-shaped, 20,000-square-meter facility in southern Belgium, inaugurated by Belgian King Philippe, will be able to produce up to 500 satellites annually, which would make it the world's third-largest satellite production site, according to the Belgian royal palace.

Employees are expected to move into the megafactory in January, and production is slated to begin by the end of 2028, according to AFP.

Satellite connectivity has become increasingly important to everyday life as well as national security.

It provides internet access to remote locations and defense equipment like warships and fighter jets, and lets governments and militaries communicate securely where Earth-based networks are unavailable or disrupted.

The importance of such capabilities has become particularly apparent in Ukraine, as the country's military relies heavily on Starlink connectivity to fight Russian forces. Recent restrictions to the service over Russian-occupied territory reported by Ukrainian officials further underscored the way geopolitical conflict may depend on commercial businesses.

But Europe still has a long way to go in challenging Starlink's dominance.

SpaceX says it has more than 11,000 Starlink satellites in low Earth orbit, making it by far the largest satellite constellation in the world, and plans up to 100,000 satellites.

Among the challengers is Amazon, whose satellite business, Amazon Leo, has deployed over 400 satellites and plans an initial in-orbit constellation of more than 3,200.

Meanwhile, French Eutelsat operates more than 600 satellites in low Earth orbit through its OneWeb constellation. The company is also part of the SpaceRISE consortium alongside SES and Hispasat, tasked with developing and operating IRIS².

The European Commission has called IRIS² a "strategic asset to achieve space sovereignty in the face of commercial satellite constellations, such as Starlink." The aim is for first launches to take place by the end of 2029, with initial services expected soon after.

In August, the Commission and SpaceRISE consortium announced they were expanding the main constellation from 282 to 348 satellites, increasing its secure communications capacity for EU governments by 60%.

Defense and security applications were the main drivers of the expanded scope, the Commission said.

#256. Rejecting cheap fabrics, higher prices: Why shoppers are asking for better clothes

Clothing isn't made like it used to be, and more shoppers are taking notice.

The makeup of apparel imported into the U.S. has shifted in recent years. In 2010, about 60% of clothing was predominantly made from cotton. Now, that total is around 45%, while predominantly synthetic fabrics such as polyester and nylon comprise the majority of imports.

Apparel prices generally declined over the two decades leading up to the pandemic, according to the consumer price index. Since 2020, they have soared to record highs.

It's starting to change habits for consumers who are paying more for food, gas, rent and other staples. As shoppers shell out more for clothes, they're increasingly making sure the garments are worth the cost.

Consulting firm AlixPartners' 2026 Consumer Sentiment Index, which surveyed more than 9,000 shoppers, found that consumers are scrutinizing labels more closely and rejecting cheap materials sold at a premium.

"If it's not a natural material, a retailer brand who passes it off as it is, they're really becoming very low on consumers' minds," said Sonia Lapinsky, head of fashion at AlixPartners.

Fast fashion brands like Forever 21 and H&M exploded in popularity during the early 2000s and 2010s, bringing trendy clothing to shelves quickly at low prices. Then came ultra-fast fashion, with brands like Shein and Fashion Nova pumping out new garments at even quicker speeds and lower price points — made possible in part through the use of inexpensive, synthetic fabrics.

"We got to a point where the pendulum just swung too far in that direction. And we've actually begun to see a reversal," said U.S. Bancorp BTIG consumer analyst Janine Stichter. "Quality matters a lot more ... over the last three or four years than it has in the previous 10 or 20."

But quality, which shoppers often tie directly to material, can come at a higher cost.

The average unit price of apparel imported to the U.S. between January and August was $2.64 per square meter for garments that were predominantly synthetic fiber, like polyester and nylon, compared with $3.67 for items that were largely cotton, according to data from the U.S. Commerce Department's Office of Textiles and Apparel.

When comparing those 2026 figures to 2010, synthetic fiber apparel import costs have climbed around 11%, while cotton apparel costs have risen 21%.

"If you're appealing to a more value-sensitive customer, you either reduce the quality, or you negotiate with suppliers to try to share some of the cost or you maybe absorb the cost … and perhaps hope to crank a higher volume of product to make up for it," said Bernstein Research senior retail analyst Aneesha Sherman.

Though polyester is cheaper, experts point out that's not necessarily a bad thing. It can help clothing hold its shape and wick away sweat.

Sherman believes the athleisure boom is a primary factor in the shift toward polyester. She said that from 2015 to 2025, sports apparel was growing at a 4.9% rate in the U.S., versus 2.6% for all apparel and footwear, according to Euromonitor data.

"There probably is some quality trade down as well happening there," Sherman said. "But I think both these trends are conflated."

Nonetheless, backlash to synthetic materials is growing. Gen Z has even repurposed the word "polyester" as an insult to mean generally low-quality or fake.

Concerns have also grown about the environment and human health surrounding petroleum-based clothing, as more research indicates microplastics can leach from polyester clothing when washed.

It's led consumers to reexamine what materials are in their clothing.

Apparel prices have risen coming out of the pandemic, as demand outpaced supply and allowed companies to raise prices, said BTIG's Stichter. Tariffs and the Iran war followed, raising costs for businesses — and prices for consumers.

Petroleum is the basis of many synthetic fibers, and oil prices have skyrocketed since the start of the Iran war.

The same factors that helped drive up prices of clothing — Covid, tariffs and war — also affected clothing quality.

"Companies had to move around their supply chains in the post-Covid period," said Stichter. "They had to find ways to take costs out, and sometimes that meant using less expensive fabrics."

Those factors compounded a more-than-decade-long shift away from manufacturing in China.

Sheng Lu, a fashion and apparel studies professor at the University of Delaware, whose research focuses on the economics of the global textile industry, said China's established history with clothing production means it has higher-skilled workers, state-of-the-art-equipment and better material management compared with emerging manufacturing destinations such as Bangladesh, Sri Lanka and Cambodia.

"They mostly serve the value market and the mass market. And I think the reason why they're not ready to make higher-quality luxury and premium products really reflects the kind of skill sets you can expect when you source from these countries," said Lu.

AlixPartners' Lapinsky said these countries that manufacture at a lower cost sometimes have to still ship the raw materials from China, which can extend the timeline to make clothing. That means taking earlier bets on trends and then discounting if products don't sell well.

"If you have to mark down that product because the consumer doesn't want it as much, your net margin might actually be worse than if you were to just invest in quality in the first place," said BTIG's Stichter.

And as material costs rise for synthetic fibers, Stichter also said she believes it could further influence companies to use more natural fabrics moving forward.

Companies prioritizing quality are winning in this environment.

Ralph Lauren's stock is up around 85% over the past two years. Around 90% of its apparel is primarily made of cotton, leather, wool, viscose, cashmere or silk, according to its 2026 sustainability report. In its fiscal 2026, the company said it sold more products at full price and reduced markdowns.

Meanwhile, direct-to-consumer clothing brand Quince, which centers its brand identity around natural materials, reached a $10 billion valuation this year.

The brand jumped 16 spots to No. 3 for best resale value in online consignment retailer ThredUp's 2025 report. Organic cotton retailer Pact jumped 20 spots on that list to No. 8, illustrating the potential role materials are playing in brand recognition.

Alternatively, the 2026 AlixPartners report, which ranks sentiment for brands in different retail categories, found shoppers are viewing some fast fashion brands less favorably. Both Shein and Zara dropped three spots — to fifth and seventh place, respectively — in terms of sentiment in the young adult specialty category.

Shein passed higher tariff costs onto shoppers in 2025 and then blamed a sales slowdown on those higher prices. The ultra-fast fashion retailer saw its year-over-year share of U.S. apparel, accessories and footwear card spending decrease in the fourth quarter of 2025 and first two quarters of 2026, according to Consumer Edge.

Shein did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

Though Zara fell in AlixPartners' Consumer Sentiment Index, it has continued to grow sales and profits year over year. Even so, Lapinsky said worsening consumer views are something to watch, even if the company is doing well.

"What we found over the years with this consumer sentiment index is that sometimes it will be a bit of a leading indicator of what's to come," said AlixPartners' Lapinsky. "I'm not saying that this is going to be true for Zara, but I think that's one thing that we'll be watching as we look in the upcoming quarters and into 2027."

#251. Latin America has the copper and energy the AI build-out needs. Infrastructure is the test

Latin America’s copper, energy and mineral resources could play a growing role in supplying the global artificial-intelligence build-out, as data centers and electrification drive demand for power and raw materials.

But for investors, it's less about whether Latin America has the resources than whether governments can turn them into viable projects.

"We are going through an acceleration in energy demand growth. We need every possible energy source available," Michele Della Vigna, head of natural resources research for Goldman Sachs, told CNBC. "Ultimately, what drives economic growth is energy," he added.

Ben Kumar, head of strategy for wealth, investment and public policy at 7IM, suggested Latin America could benefit, given the physical materials needed for AI equipment and data centers. "We are currently neutral in the short term, but if the materials and mining cycle continues, we could see this moving to positive in a few months," he said.

The investment case varies across the region. Chile has copper; Argentina is expanding oil and gas exports; and Brazil has abundant renewable power and significant rare earth reserves.

Chile's copper sector illustrates both the supply potential and the constraints to expanding supply. Copper production fell 8.5% in the first nine months of 2026, according to the state copper commission Cochilco, as lower ore grades, maintenance, and operational disruptions constrained supply.

But demand for refined copper is set to grow 1.6% this year and 2% next year, according to the International Copper Study Group. Supply is struggling to keep pace with demand from expanding electricity grids.

Chile's copper commission has raised its 2027 price forecast to $5.95 a pound, from $5.10. Codelco, the state-owned copper producer, may also have scope to increase supply, including through proposed public-private partnerships.

Rafael Ch, partner and senior analyst for Latin America and emerging markets at Signum Global Advisors, sees demand continuing to grow. "As countries expand electricity grids, they need more copper regardless of which power source they choose," he said.

The scale of the opportunity will depend in part on how quickly energy demand grows. Goldman Sachs forecasts that global power demand for data centers will grow by 170% by 2030. The IEA projected in April that global data center electricity consumption would rise from 485 terawatt-hours in 2025 to 950 TWh in 2030.

The investment case also depends on whether the region can capture more value from the resources it produces. Latin America and the Caribbean refined only 26% of the copper they mined in 2025, according to the IEA.

"Cautious optimism is justified and there will be massive critical mineral demand," said Monica de Bolle, senior fellow at the Peterson Institute for International Economics.

"But improving refining capacity will be crucial to capture more of that value," she added.

Some projects have already secured financing. Chilean explorer Marimaca Copper Corp selected a preferred lender for a $400 million debt facility to build a mine on Oct. 28. The funding will go towards developing an open-pit mine in the north of Chile that will produce 50,000 metric tons of refined copper annually. Production is expected from 2029.

Argentina shows the other side of the opportunity: strong oil and gas resources, but insufficient infrastructure to get more production to global markets.

"I see an opportunity in the infrastructure that gets Argentine oil and gas to market. A producer in Vaca Muerta can have excellent wells and still struggle to expand exports without pipeline capacity and access to ports.

That gives investors a reason to look at transport and export infrastructure alongside production," Rafael Ch, partner and senior analyst for Latin America and emerging markets at Signum Global Advisors, added. Vaca Muerta is Argentina's vast shale basin.

Argentina is also attracting investment in drilling and export infrastructure. International capital markets have reopened to investment in drilling and pipelines in Argentina. That investment has already driven the build-out of a pipeline and port. Argentina will then be able to export far more crude oil. Chevron and American oil tycoon Harold Hamm have also increased their investments in Argentina.

Brazil's case rests partly on its renewable-heavy power mix. About 89% of the country's electricity was generated from renewable sources in 2025, according to the country's Ministry of Mines and Energy.

"That puts Brazil in a strong position to benefit as AI and data centers drive up demand," Stephanie Link, chief investment strategist and portfolio manager at Hightower Advisors, told CNBC.

But while Brazil has the world's second-largest reported rare-earth reserves, its estimated mine production is far smaller than China's. USGS estimates put Brazil's output at 2,000 metric tons in 2025, compared with China's 270,000 metric tons. Brazil's reserves are estimated at 11 million metric tons by the USGS, second only to China's 44 million metric tons.

For investors, the gap between Brazil's vast reserves and limited current production is both the opportunity and the execution risk.

For investors, the policy question is whether governments can make projects viable and keep them on track.

Luis Costa, Citi's global head of emerging markets strategy, said governments need to offer incentives, particularly in the early stages of projects, because infrastructure lags the region's resource wealth. He also stressed that support must last: infrastructure projects can take several years to develop.

The World Bank's Latin America and the Caribbean Economic Update for 2026 projects growth for the region of 2.2%, and highlights the "restricted fiscal space, sticky inflation and high borrowing costs."

"The economic policy and fiscal policy needs to get in line as markets are globally becoming more sensitive to fiscal stories," said Costa.

Brazil will need to establish fiscal credibility to attract investment, particularly if higher public spending puts additional pressure on debt and borrowing costs. "If investors doubt the government can stabilize public debt, companies' long-term financing costs could stay high even as the central bank lowers its policy rate," Ch added.

With Argentina set for national elections in October 2027, political continuity will also matter for energy investment. "A return to tighter currency controls or persistent deficits would make companies more likely to delay projects, even in an oil field with attractive economics," said Ch.

Pro-business President Javier Milei faces a tight race for re-election, though a change in leadership would not necessarily undermine medium- to long-term investment.

"There is also the possibility of a pragmatic Peronist ticket emerging, which markets would be satisfied with," said Nicolas Jaquier, emerging-market fixed-income portfolio manager at Ninety One.

For those governing across Latin America, policies to unlock its wealth of resources will be key for it to emerge as a winner from the AI demand boom.

"Supply chain security is becoming more and more relevant, and if commodities take off Latin America can be a clear and obvious winner," said Kumar.

#247. Ex-GM CEO on Mary Barra succession planning: 'It's going to be tough'

Former General Motors CEO and Chairman Dan Akerson doesn't mince words when asked about the challenges the automaker faces in finding a successor for GM CEO Mary Barra: "It's going to be tough."

He should know. The 78-year-old businessman and U.S. Navy veteran helped with his own succession planning and recommended to GM's board in 2013 that Barra replace him as CEO. She has held the top job since Jan. 15, 2014.

Akerson said Barra will be hard to replace, citing her success and prominence as the first female CEO of any major automaker and the ongoing challenges facing the automotive industry, such as artificial intelligence, vehicle digitalization, and the rise of China and autonomous vehicles.

"It's going to be very, very dynamic," Akerson told CNBC during a phone interview. "It's going to be a big change."

Akerson declined to discuss potential internal candidates such as GM Chief Product Officer Sterling Anderson, CFO Paul Jacobson or President Mark Reuss, but he said the eventual successor needs to be dynamic and, if from the outside, "adaptable."

His comments to CNBC follow a Wall Street Journal report on Tuesday that GM is working with an executive search firm to identify possible outside candidates to succeed Barra, in addition to internal prospects. GM declined to comment on the report or Barra's succession.

Akerson, who came to GM as an outsider from The Carlyle Group in 2010, said selecting an outside candidate might be risky if there are already executives within the organization who have similar leadership and technical expertise.

"Is there anybody in the organization that has that background that you know better day to day over a period of time? You may be taking a risk by bringing in an outsider," he said.

The prospect of an outsider leading GM would be unusual in the auto industry, where major automakers have generally promoted internal CEO candidates, apart from periods of exceptional disruption such as the Great Recession.

Stellantis, BMW, Toyota Motor, Nissan Motor and Hyundai Motor have all appointed internal executives to their CEO positions in recent years.

Barra has said several times that her successor and her longevity in the role would be determined by the board.

A notable influence is likely Hewlett Packard veteran Patricia Russo, GM's longest-serving board member who has been on the board since 2009 and leads its CEO succession planning as independent lead director.

GM's CEO succession planning is done "at least annually," according to the company's yearly proxy filing. That process includes Barra providing the board "with recommendations for, and evaluations of, potential CEO successors and discusses developmental plans for these successors," according to the filing.

That is followed by board members engaging "with CEO candidates and senior management talent at Board and committee meetings and other forums to enable directors to personally assess candidates."

When Akerson was preparing to retire, Barra was among a pool of four candidates — two longtime GM employees and two relative newcomers — who were finalists for CEO in 2013, he said.

Akerson said he put the finalists through an extended evaluation, asking how they viewed GM's strengths and weaknesses, how they would organize the company, and their willingness to work together, potentially with other CEO candidates who didn't get the job.

Akerson said the final decision to appoint Barra was done at a day-and-a-half-long board meeting in Washington. The candidate field was narrowed to Barra and former GM executive Dan Ammann, an ex-Wall Street banker who was GM's CFO before leaving the automaker's Cruise unit, reportedly over a disagreement in strategy with Barra, in 2021.

"Mary's very deserving of all the accolades she's received," Akerson said. "She's done a terrific job."

Barra has been leading the automaker for nearly 13 years, which makes her the longest-serving CEO of the automaker since Alfred Sloan retired after a 23-year career as CEO in 1946.

Akerson said he believes that, like he did, Barra will have major influence in picking her successor. He described that dynamic as integral to continuing her legacy leading the automaker.

"This is important. I think if she doesn't pick the right person, in four or five years from now and things are back in the trough, that will tarnish her history. But I think she'll pick the right one," he said.

#244. S&P warns Nvidia credit rating could be cut over 'rising' management and governance risk

Nvidia's credit rating could be cut by one notch over the next 90 days if the company continues to have "deficiencies" in its management and governance framework, S&P Global Ratings said in the wake of the Jensen Huang's ouster and reinstatement as Nvidia CEO.

"News regarding the CEO and questions regarding potential export control violations have exposed vulnerabilities in Nvidia's management and governance framework, especially in regard to its board structure, composition, and effectiveness," S&P said in a Wednesday note.

Nvidia currently holds an AA rating, the third-highest level.

S&P has placed the chipmaker on "CreditWatch with negative implications," though any potential downgrade would be limited to one notch, to AA-, unless investigations or disclosures lead the agency to conclude that Nvidia's reputation or future prospects have also been harmed.

A downgrade can pressure bond prices, push yields higher and raise borrowing costs. Nvidia priced $25 billion of investment-grade senior notes in June, with maturities from 2028 through 2056, shortly after S&P upgraded the company from AA- to AA.

Nvidia has about $32.5 billion of bonds in the U.S. high-grade corporate bond index, according to Bloomberg.

S&P's Wednesday note follows Moody's Ratings changing its outlook on Nvidia to stable from positive on Nov. 18, citing governance concerns, and leaving that outlook unchanged after Huang's return on Monday.

In its note, S&P cited the "long tenure" of certain board members as a reason their independence may be "compromised" and said Nvidia's management and governance risk was "rising."

The ratings agency added that it remained unaware of any remedial action taken by the board to address what could be "meaningful" management and governance deficiencies.

Chairman Harvey Jones and early investor Tench Coxe have served on Nvidia's board since 1993, while Brooke Seawell joined in 1997. Mark Stevens served from 1993 to 2006 and rejoined the board in 2008.

S&P also flagged questions around geopolitical risk management and internal controls arising from Nvidia's exposure to China.

"While Nvidia management has delivered significant returns for its stakeholders over decades, weak executive oversight and lack of succession planning have brought questions around the "key man" risk to the forefront," S&P's Andrew Chang and David Tsui said.

Chang and Tsui noted that Huang's resignation and subsequent reinstatement did not resolve questions about the board's oversight of executive conduct.

The agency also intends to meet with Nvidia's board and management to discuss plans to address those deficiencies, particularly around "board independence, effective management oversight and a credible leadership succession plan."

Huang returned less than two weeks after Jones said Nvidia's directors did not intend to reinstate him. The board later voted unanimously to bring Huang back, sending Nvidia shares up as much as 36% during Monday's session.

Nvidia shares are down about 12.5% since Monday's close.

#243. Why Gen Z are planning for life without a state pension

Why Gen Z are planning for life without a state pension

Joel has finally landed his first graduate engineering job after several years of lowerâpaid roles. He's in his early 20s, lives with his parents and works in London. But instead of splashing the extra cash, or saving up for holidays or a house deposit, he's decided to squirrel more of it away into his workplace pension.

The reason? He doesn't think he'll get any kind of state pension. Like Joel, around half of Gen Z (those born from 1997â2012) say they don't expect the state pension to exist by the time they retire. It's pretty stark to hear, but growing up with constant headlines about an ageing population, a proportionally smaller working-age population, and the pressure that government finances are under, Joel thinks it's his generation that will suffer.

"I don't believe that I'll be a recipient of a state pension. I know a lot of people my age don't think they're going to be... There just won't be enough money," he says.

Retirement has always felt distant when you're in your 20s - something to think about later. But what's emerging among today's underâ30s is something different: not just distance, but doubt.

"It just mathematically doesn't make senseâ¦ There has to get to a point where that state pension is taking up too much of the budget and can't exist in the way that it exists right now," Joel says.

"I don't believe that I'll be a recipient of a state pension," says Joel

The state pension age is shifting. At the start of April, the age at which you receive it began to gradually creep up, rising from 66 years to 67 years by March 2028. It's due to go up again in 20 years' time to 68, though that might happen earlier as the government has an ongoing independent review.

That's a frustration for 27-year-old retail manager Connor, who got in touch via BBC Your Voice, because he says "the goalpost keeps moving". "At the minute I'll be 68 by the time I can retire, but I do think I'll be probably closer to 75, if I'm honest."

More than 13 million people - 19% of the population - are currently of state pension age. By 2050, even with the state pension age rising to 68, that group is projected to exceed 15 million people, nearly a quarter of the population, with numbers projected to climb towards 17 million by the 2070s. In other words, there will be lots more people qualifying for the state pension, and fewer working people, as a proportion, paying taxes into the pot to cover the bill.

At the same time, almost half of workingâage adults are not paying into a private pension pot. That means many will be relying solely on the state pension for their retirement income - and with relative poverty rates among pensioners now at 14%, we can already see how difficult that can be.

Experts warn that if a whole generation stops believing the state pension will be there, it could push people towards more risky investments, prompt overly restrictive behaviour, or lead others not to save at all.

So, are we heading towards a major pension crisis for many in Gen Z? And if we are, might the Gen Z generation end up redefining what retirement looks like?

For those hitting the state pension milestone today, as long as they've made 35 years of National Insurance contributions, they're entitled to Â£241.30 a week.

That amount rises each year to help people keep pace with rising living costs. Since 2011, pension increases have been guaranteed by the triple lock - which means the rise will either match the rate of inflation, average earnings or 2.5% - whichever is higher.

But in recent weeks, several organisations have called for the rules to be rewritten.

The centre-left Resolution Foundation think tank has argued for scrapping the triple lock, saying that continuing to prioritise the incomes of pensioners over working-age adults and children would be unfair.

Meanwhile, the Tony Blair Institute (TBI), a think tank set up by the former prime minister, takes things a step further and has called for the whole state pension to be scrapped and replaced with a new "Lifespan Fund". Thomas Smith, director of economic policy at the TBI, argued: "Britain's state pension system was built for a different era. We can't keep pouring money into a system that is increasingly unaffordable."

It suggests scrapping the triple lock and allowing people to access some of their state pension early if they need it because of redundancy and frequent job changes.

That's an idea that might appeal to Connor in Chesterfield. He's facing redundancy from his job at a global cosmetics firm, and the ability to draw down a small amount - effectively a withdrawal from his future state pension - might tide him through.

"There's not that many jobs out there at the minute, unfortunately. I still live at home with my parents luckily, but I pay board to them. I still have a car payment, I still have my insurance to pay."

"There's not that many jobs out there," says Connor

But former pensions minister Steve Webb argues those changes would be "a huge backward step". He says the benefit of the current system is its simplicity which shouldn't be replaced with "something fiendishly complex and highly intrusive, which would take many decades to implement in full."

The government says it has committed to the triple lock for the rest of this parliament, and that the Pensions Commission, an independent body set up to review the regime for UK private pensions, is examining "how we can ensure secure retirements for tomorrow's pensioners".

It's likely that those in their 20s will not have a tripleâlocked pension. That means living on the state pension alone will become more difficult, as its value may rise more slowly than the cost of food, travel, clothing and household bills.

For those who doubt the triple lock will endure, the debate often shifts to how the state pension can be sustained at all. One idea that regularly surfaces is meansâtesting. In some ways it already is: very lowâincome pensioners can receive an additional benefit called Pension Credit. But 24âyearâold Joel believes that for the state pension to survive, the choices may need to be more radical.

"I don't think a means-tested state pension is necessarily a bad thing. But it would be a bad thing if it only applies to people in 50 years and not now when we should be saving some of that money."

Engineer Joel is one of life's squirrels. His fear about the future of the state pension means he's doubling-down on his private pension. He contacted BBC Your Voice because he feels that consecutive governments have sheltered current pensioners, leaving the consequences for his generation to face.

"I'm going to have to increase the amount of my paycheck that goes into a private pension, which obviously isn't good with cost of living through the roof," he says.

The scale of what younger workers may need to save adds to that anxiety for some. Investment company Rathbones estimates that a single person retiring today at 65 (with the state pension) may need around Â£796,000 in savings to fund a "comfortable retirement". If the state pension remains, a 25âyearâold today would need a pot of around Â£1.68m to retire comfortably as a single person. Without the state pension, the figure for Gen Z jumps to more than Â£2.4m.

Against this backdrop, Joel says many of his friends are considering opting out of private and workplace pensions altogether and investing independently instead, mostly in "crypto or index funds and things like that.

"There's a sense, whether it's right or wrong, that that's more secure than putting it in a pension where they're also going to take a chip on top."

It's possible that individual investment choices could earn more than a pension scheme, but it's a big gamble.

Behavioural economics suggests that when people lose trust in a system, they tend to either opt out entirely or overâcompensate. Both can be problematic. Saving extra in a private pension may limit current life options, but opting out can leave many with riskier retirement savings, or indeed none at all.

How the social media ban could reshape how all of us use the internet

The pressure on the Church of England to ditch its slavery reparations plan

What one country's experiment says about attempts to boost birth rates

In central Manchester, 23âyearâold Ashleigh agrees with Joel that the state pension is unlikely to be coming her way: "At this rate I don't think anyone's ever going to retire, I think everyone will just have to fend for themselves in the end."

But as someone on a lower income, her pension choices are less squirrelâlike. When working for a big retailer, she says that she chose to stop contributing to her employer's auto-enrolment pension.

"I opted out of it. I need the money now." She explains: "I'd rather save for a house and then at least I have something to show for it".

Some experts warn that the gap between rich and poor in retirement could widen significantly for this generation.

Dr Suzy Morrissey, deputy director at the Pensions Policy Institute (PPI), believes that alongside how much Gen Z save privately, another factor will widen the divide: far more of them will be renting.

"Renting in retirement increases your chances of pensioner poverty, and they do face challenges to save, as younger people, that previous generations didn't face when they were at the same age," she says. "If we have people paying rent in retirement who don't have large pension pots to cover those expenses, then that equals higher risk of pensioner poverty."

But Morrissey sees a silver lining: pensions auto-enrollment, the system that automatically puts most employees into a workplace pension unless they opt out. If they've been employees, "they will have spent their working life contributing into a pension pot, and they will be the first generation that will have spent their whole life doing that."

It'll be a backstop for many, but the minimum contribution rate is unlikely to be enough for a comfortable retirement. It's not automatic for the self-employed and people like Ashleigh have opted out because of immediate financial pressures, so it looks like plenty won't see the benefit of that silver lining.

For some, the response to an uncertain future is to focus on the present.

Lauren, from Hull, says: "Money always comes back, time doesn't. The world is so vast, we shouldn't wait till the last 10/20 years of our lives to go out and see it!"

At 24, she's about to take six months off from her job as a business coordinator. She's one of a growing number planning to take regular career breaks, or "grown-up gap years", which many are terming "mini retirement". HSBC's 2025 UK survey found that 63% of Gen Z plan to take at least one miniâretirement, compared with 32% of Gen X and 13% of Boomers.

"The majority of my friends don't pay into pensions and instead decide to take their whole wage [after taxes] and spend it how they see fit. A large proportion goes on travels or holidays," says Lauren.

"I currently don't pay into a pension, actually I never have. I'd way rather have my money now and use it to live life," she says.

"The majority of my friends don't pay into pensions," says Lauren

But there's a warning for Gen Z from the experience of the Waspi women - hundreds of thousands born in the 1950s who campaigners say have suffered because of poorly communicated rises in the state pension age. Their financial shock shows that costs may only become clear when it's too late to course-correct.

If Gen Z's suspicions are right, and the state pension becomes a less dependable part of income in later life, then more will have to take a totally different approach to retirement, savings and life choices to navigate the new landscape.

Additional reporting: Kris Bramwell and Harriet Whitehead

#240. Why the UK is dithering over what to do about e-scooters

Why the UK is dithering over what to do about e-scooters

When I step out of Southampton Central railway station, it takes me just seconds to spot an e-scooter. In this city, these devices have become a normal part of the transport ecosystem, along with e-bikes.

"It's just the most convenient way to commute from home to the station and back," says Keith Reed, who I speak to as he arrives at the station to catch a train to London. Keith says uses rental e-scooters twice a week. His alternative option would be his bike, but he'd then have to worry about where to leave it.

Around the city, there are parking areas for bright orange rental devices everywhere - but they're not necessarily a permanent feature. Southampton hosts one of the numerous government-backed trials around England. This one has been going on since 2021.

A study suggests around half of users of e-scooter do so in conjunction with another mode of transport - like Keith, who rides to the railway station

It also doesn't take long to find e-scooters which aren't part of the trial, but instead are owned by individuals. I see a man riding one along the pavement outside a parade of shops. He doesn't want to stop and talk.

These privately owned e-scooters are illegal to ride on public roads across the UK, but they've proliferated regardless. One expert told me the situation was a "Wild West".

Among people I spoke to in Southampton, the same concerns came up time and time again: "They're a blinking nuisance," says elderly pedestrian Fred. Another shopper, Martin, says he's "very concerned" whenever he sees them racing through precincts: "An accident's almost inevitable."

The endgame of the trials is to reach a decision on whether to make permanent hired e-scooter schemes such as the one Keith uses - and whether to bring in legislation that would legalise and regulate privately owned e-scooters too.

But six years since the trials started around England, there's still no conclusion - and as the UK government has responsibility for road traffic legislation across Great Britain, officials in Scotland and Wales say they are waiting on Westminster to act.

So why is it taking so long for the government to decide what to do, and what are the consequences of delay? What does the evidence tell us about how safe these things are?

And why are people using them in the first place - are they here to stay as a useful part of the transport offer, or will they end up being viewed as a pest on our streets?

You'd recognise an e-scooter if you saw one. The Department for Transport's (DfT's) definition of an e-scooter includes no pedals, two wheels and a maximum continuous power rating of 500W.

And so far, the trials around England appear to have proved popular. According to DfT research, fom January 2022 to May 2024, 36.9 million trips were made across 30 trial areas outside of London. On average, nearly 19,500 e-scooters were available to hire every day.

Dr Yasir Ali, a reader in Transport Modelling at Loughborough University, says e-scooters are "complementary" to other modes of transport, rather than replacing them. Due to factors such as range and being exposed to the elements, they are less suitable for longer trips, rural areas with less infrastructure or in bad weather. They work best as "a first- and last-mile solution" in urban areas, to reduce reliance on cars for short trips.

Shared mobility charity CoMoUK's 2025 report suggests that for more than half of the people using rental e-scooters, the most common use was travelling to work or school. Its analysis of DfT data suggests the majority of people using hired e-scooters are male and under 35.

"Sometimes it's a bit difficult to take the bus from dot to dot", says Yan Zhang, who works at Southampton university and doesn't drive, "so for shorter distances, I'll take the scooter."

Like Yan Zhang, e-scooter users are less likely to have a car, CoMo's report says - this is partly explained by official trials' concentration in urban areas

Around half of respondents to CoMoUK's online survey used it in conjunction with another mode of transport - such as riding to a railway station, like Keith.

Earlier evidence from a national evaluation of the trials published in 2022 found that although some users would have driven instead had they not had access to an e-scooter, the biggest "modal shift" - meaning switch in ways of getting around - to e-scooters was from walking. Given walking is an active mode of travel, this has raised questions over how significant the public health impact of e-scooter use really is.

And the usage of privately owned e-scooters isn't reflected in this research. The DfT has said between 760,000 and 1.2 million people in England aged 16 and over own one - despite the fact that it remains illegal to ride them in public.

In the eyes of the law, e-scooters are classed as motor vehicles. Outside of the trials, they can't be insured, and thus can only legally be ridden on private land, with the owner's permission.

Parking bays for e-scooters and e-bikes are available across Southampton

The trials themselves are bound by strict rules. The devices' top speed is limited. Businesses who operate the pilots must insure them. Users must have a full or provisional driving licence, and they must not ride on the pavement. Helmets are recommended but not mandatory.

Richard Dilks, CEO of CoMoUK says "mass-scale use of something that is inherently low-carbon, low-impact, low-cost" is a "good thing" but "we also don't have rules of engagement about how they should be ridden".

And as a result of the delays in introducing regulation, Loughborogh University's Yasir Ali says "we are increasingly in a situation where the law and reality have diverged", with "the disadvantages of e-scooter use without securing the full benefits of regulation".

Back in Southampton, I meet a lady pushing a black e-scooter, who doesn't want to give her name. She says nobody told her when she bought it that it was illegal to ride on the streets.

The Driver and Vehicle Standards Agency has a unit that carries out surveillance of retailers to check they're complying with the law. But earlier this month, an investigation by the Press Association news agency discovered some retailers were advertising them for commuting purposes.

Police tell me they regularly see children illegally riding private e-scooters. Margaret Winchcomb, deputy executive director at the Parliamentary Advisory Council for Transport Safety (PACTS), says some are being specifically marketed at kids.

Police say they will seize privately owned e-scooters that are ridden illegally

So what can police do? Forces regularly launch crackdowns where they seize illegally used devices, and often crush them. Each year, awareness campaigns run urging parents not to buy their kids an e-scooter for Christmas. But enforcement is a challenge.

Insp Matt Boiles from Avon and Somerset's road policing team says problems arise even with the legal trial devices - for example, people riding them after drinking alcohol, which counts as drink-driving. Riding with another person on the back is another common issue.

But officers see private e-scooters being illegally ridden in public places "all the time, every single day" and they can be really hard to catch - it isn't always safe for police in a car to pursue someone on an e-scooter, he says.

Insp Boiles despairs over parents buying them for children: "We do have parents attend a police station quite a lot, getting very, very upset that their child's Â£500 e-scooter has been seized." But "the legislation is really, really clear that you can't have it back".

There's a potential risk of fire if unregulated devices with lithium batteries are left to charge unattended. Most train operators have banned e-scooters on board for this reason.

But there's also the fact that riders are unprotected in the case of falling off or being hit by motorised vehicles. And - along with e-bikes - they can end up involved in collisions where pedestrians are hurt. Many trial e-scooters in the UK are restricted to 12.5mph, but privately owned scooters' speeds aren't.

Some coroners have even raised concerns. In 2022, a coroner in London called for action after the death of 14-year-old Fatima Abukah on a privately owned scooter.

According to the DfT, in 2024 - the most recent year for which figures are available - 1,390 casualties were recorded in collisions that involved e-scooter in England, Scotland and Wales, three more than in the previous 12 months. The vast majority, 1,096, were e-scooter users themselves.

Young boys and men appear to be over-represented in the stats. Of the casualties, 302 - the most of any age and sex category - were males aged 10-19.

Six deaths were recorded in 2024, unchanged from the previous year. Five of these were riders and one a pedestrian.

Although the numbers aren't conclusive, it is thought more accidents are happening on privately owned e-scooters. Winchcomb says police statistics "aren't reflective of the number of injuries".

Nonetheless, campaigners believe there is enough evidence to show that regulation is urgently needed.

Carly Calland's 14-year-old son Jacob died in March 2025 of a catastrophic head injury. He was a passenger on an e-scooter that was involved with a collision with a car.

If privately owned e-scooters are legalised for public use, Carly believes, there should be mandatory helmets, a ban on carrying passengers and penalties for parents that allow children to ride illegally.

"If Jacob was wearing a helmet that day, he would still be here," Carly, from Wythenshawe in Greater Manchester, says.

Carly is not against e-scooters. They "are really good for people to get to work, and they are eco-friendly", she tells me, but "they just need to be used in the correct way."

What happens if the current situation goes on? Her answer is emphatic: "More deaths."

Meanwhile a number of major European cities have ended their shared e-scooter schemes. Paris abolished them after a referendum in 2023. Brussels is the most recent, announcing theirs would end in 2027, following a rise in accidents.

The last evaluation of the trials in England was in 2022. New findings are expected by early next year.

In recent years, successive British transport secretaries have supported the idea of a new law to legalise and regulate the e-scooters available for sale. E-scooters were mentioned in the 2022 Queen's speech, but nothing materialised.

The hidden cost of the night shift and how to sleep it off

After record heat, could the Atlantic make Britain's weather even more extreme?

We are living fewer years in good health: Is the NHS part of the problem?

Winchcomb says some of the "dithering" may be because of the potential knock-on effect of any legislation for users of mobility scooters.

There is some frustration from devolved administrations around the UK about the delays in Whitehall. The Scottish Government "will continue to urge the UK government, which holds powers on registration and licensing over off-road vehicles, to take action in this area", a spokesperson says.

The Welsh Government says it will "monitor developments on legalisation plans and consider the implications for Wales".

Northern Ireland's Department for Infrastructure says it is also monitoring the trials in England, as well as the impact of recent law changes in the Republic of Ireland.

The DfT says it says the trials are "building the real-world evidence needed to get regulation right" and it will consult on longer-term rules, including registration and licensing requirements, within the next year.

"E-scooters have real potential to improve local connectivity and cut emissions, and we want to see that potential realised safely and responsibly across the UK," a spokesperson says.

But Ali says: "The current halfway house becomes harder to justify as usage grows."

Even if legislation and associated technical standards were introduced, Richard Dilks acknowledges enforcing them would remain difficult.

But, he adds: "That problem only gets bigger if you leave it."

#237. Aviation faces hotter, stormier skies – and passengers might have to accept more disruption

Aviation faces hotter, stormier skies â and passengers might have to accept more disruption

As the country sweltered through one of the many heatwaves of a baking summer in late June, towering thunderclouds were gathering over the south of England and across much of northern Europe. It was a Saturday, and with the holiday season getting under way, thousands of passengers were heading to London's Heathrow and Gatwick airports to catch flights abroad. It was always going to be a very busy day.

What followed, however, was chaos for travellers. As air traffic controllers struggled to route aircraft around the storms, airspace became heavily congested and many planes due to fly were simply forced to wait on the ground. In the end, some 900 flights to and from both airports were delayed, some by up to 11 hours. Dozens more were cancelled. At airports across Europe, passengers expecting to fly into London also faced long waits and uncertainty.

The weather in the UK may now have cooled, and passengers may soon forget their experiences. But some within the industry believe what happened in June should be seen as a warning.

The timing in this case was clearly unlucky. But there are concerns that such incidents are only going to become more common as the effects of climate change mount up around the world and weather systems are likely to become increasingly unstable, making disruption more likely and adding to costs for airlines, airports and travellers.

Tim Atkinson, an airline risk consultant and former pilot, says the very crowded skies over the UK and northern Europe mean the region is particularly exposed.

He thinks there is a real risk, for example, that all of London's airports could be affected by storms at the same time, potentially with major consequences in the air and on the ground.

"What we're seeing now is very rapid climate change," he says.

"We're going from the kind of weather activity which takes out an airport, and which the system kind of copes withâ¦ to a situation where, with very little predictability, it would be possible to lose all of the London airports from real-time operation quite swiftly and with little notice".

Such a scenario, he thinks, would make the disruption for passengers caused by the incident in June seem relatively minor â and could even put lives at risk. But how likely is it?

For controllers at National Air Traffic Services's (NATS) main control centre in Swanwick, Hampshire, thunderstorms are a major preoccupation. When I visit, it is a hot and humid day, there are ominous dark clouds in the sky, and heavy, thundery downpours are hitting the surrounding region.

In Terminal Control, the part of the building responsible for flights heading to or from airports in London and southern England, those same storm cells are being monitored continuously. Their progress is tracked on large radar screens, while at the same time weather forecasts are carefully scrutinised.

Air traffic tends to come in waves during the day. Right now, at lunchtime, it is relatively quiet and the storms have yet to affect major routes. But with activity expected to intensify in the afternoon, staff are already preparing for what could be a tricky few hours.

Stormy weather can shrink the available airspace, meaning traffic has to be restricted and rerouted. If planes are unable to land at one airport, for example due to high winds, they may need diverting to another. But that can create its own problems; if too many unscheduled aircraft land at an already busy airport, it might not have sufficient infrastructure to accommodate them all.

Heavy rain and storms can make flying harder

Planes need fuel and somewhere to park. Passengers need food and facilities. Ultimately, if there isn't space, it can simply refuse to accept all but emergency traffic.

This is where the scenario set out by Atkinson comes in. He warns of a situation where traffic is diverted from Heathrow, Gatwick, Luton and Stansted at the same time, leaving aircraft coming in across the Atlantic and a large number of European flights all looking for somewhere to land at the same time, and some with dwindling fuel reserves.

This would at the very least create serious disruption for passengers, who might end up hundreds of miles from their destinations. But it could also, he thinks, create "a very serious risk event, such as an aircraft running out of fuel in the sky".

Aircraft are legally required to carry enough fuel to take them to an alternative airport if necessary, and to allow them to fly for an extra 30 minutes on top of that.

Not everyone shares Atkinson's concern. Former airline executive Andrew Charlton, who manages the Swiss-based consultancy Aviation Advocacy, thinks the risk is low.

"You've always got to imagine the worst-case scenario", he says. "But I also think we need to keep that worst case in context.

"Yes, it's possible to imagine it. But I'm also fairly comfortable with the ability of this industry to react well and make good decisionsâ¦ of all the things that wake me up at 3amâ¦this is not the top of that list."

Thunderstorms are caused by what is known as convective activity, where moist air close to the ground warms rapidly and rises quickly. As it rises, it expands and cools, allowing the moisture it carries to condense, forming large clouds. More heat means more convective energy, potentially creating instability. In addition, for every degree the Earth warms, the atmosphere can hold about 7% more moisture, increasing the probability of heavy rainfall.

It is not clear whether this means we will actually get more thunderstorms in the UK and across Europe, as so many variables are involved beyond the simple matter of energy and moisture. Some parts of the world may even get fewer. However, studies have shown that higher latitudes, particularly in the northern hemisphere, will see the biggest increases in favourable conditions for storms to form, and those that do may be more violent. According to the UK's Met Office, "while we may not see more thunderstorms overall, the ones we do experience could be more impactful".

Where possible, pilots will avoid flying through convective weather. Even though aircraft are designed to cope with severe conditions, storms can produce intense turbulence, capable of damaging the structural frame of the plane, as well as throwing people around the cabin if they are not strapped in. Hail can also be a big problem, potentially cracking windscreens or causing engine problems. So, however much inconvenience it may cause passengers, crews will invariably put safety first.

New technology helps pilots navigate tricky flying conditions

Onboard weather radars can help aircrew to see up to 320 nautical miles ahead. They also have access to live weather forecasting resources online. But thunderstorms can move quickly and, according to Prof Guy Gratton, an expert in aeronautics at Cranfield University, these systems are essentially "tactical rather than strategic". In other words, although they enable pilots to see storm cells on their screens from a relatively short distance away, and request a diversion, they do not allow for detailed long-distance planning.

The result is that unplanned diversions often happen late in flight, which is likely to mean burning more fuel. If storm delays do become more common, says Andrew Charlton, then aircraft will have to load more fuel as a matter of course, in case there is trouble brewing ahead. "Aircraft are going to have to fly further, and they're going to have to factor in the assumption they're going to fly further," he says. For an airline, fuel is one of its major costs â and if the fuel bill goes up, he says, it is inevitable passengers will pay more.

More intense storms would be likely to make air traffic management in busy regions harder and potentially lead to more delays and cancellations. However, it is possible some of this could be mitigated using new technologies.

Both Heathrow and Gatwick, for example, have moved away from mandating minimum distances between incoming aircraft in favour of a system which continually calculates spacing times based on wind speeds and aircraft types. In practice, this is meant to allow controllers to reduce the distance between landing aircraft, particularly in high winds, and get them on the ground more quickly. According to NATS, it has successfully improved landing rates and reduced delays by a significant margin.

However, there could be other effects from more severe storms as well. Heavier rainfall, for instance, increases the likelihood of flooding. In a worst case, this can paralyse airport operations, as happened in April 2024 when torrential downpours hit Dubai International Airport. It briefly had to close its runways, while disruption lasted for two days. More than 1,200 flights were cancelled, affecting tens of thousands of passengers.

Passengers were delayed during the floods in Dubai

But even if runways and taxiways themselves are clear, and aircraft are able to fly without any difficulty, passengers may struggle to catch their flights if roads, railways or car parks are flooded.

Protecting against such risks can be expensive. Gatwick Airport, which experienced severe flooding over the Christmas period in 2013, has spent tens of millions of pounds on mitigation strategies in recent years â although those figures are dwarfed by the more than Â£2bn it is expecting to invest in its current expansion plans.

The problem of clear-air turbulence is also a real one. It cannot be seen, and is becoming increasingly common as the climate warms.

This is when air moves violently at high altitudes with no clouds or other visual cues to help a pilot avoid it. For passengers, it usually only means a little bit of bumpiness, but occasionally it can be a lot more serious. On 1 March 2023, for example, a Lufthansa flight from Austin, Texas to Frankfurt encountered what the airline described as "brief but severe" turbulence.

Those aboard described how passengers and flight crew were thrown around the cabin as the plane suddenly lost altitude. After it made an emergency landing in Washington DC, seven people were taken to hospital.

While still rare, this kind of turbulence is already much more prevalent than it used to be and is likely to become even more so.

"You know the projections are pretty dire: doubling, trebling or worse in the amount of severe turbulence on many busy flight routes around the world," says Paul Williams, a professor of atmospheric science at the University of Reading. He says incidents over the North Atlantic have increased by 55% since 1979.

Nevertheless, he remains confident solutions can be found, including improvements to forecasting and making use of laser-based technology.

There is another climate-related threat to aviation, which may prove challenging to overcome: rising sea levels. When Hurricane Sandy arrived off the US East Coast in October 2012, it provoked a storm surge that flooded the city. The two runways at New York's LaGuardia airport were left under water for several days, preventing it from reopening even after the winds had subsided.

Significant flooding that would have affected the New York City area once in every 500 years in pre-industrial times is now experienced every 25 years or so â and could occur every five years by the middle of the century, according to one major study. That makes LaGuardia, built on reclaimed land next to Flushing Bay, appear highly vulnerable.

LaGuardia could be at greater risk of flooding and future closures

It is not alone. With some 40% of the world's population living within 100km of coastlines, many airports have been built on low-lying areas near the sea. According to research by Newcastle University, 269 airports around the world are already at risk of flooding due to rising sea levels, and that total is expected to rise. Among those potentially most exposed are the likes of Amsterdam Schiphol, London City, Bahrain International and Newark Liberty International.

Many airports do already have some degree of flood protection, especially larger ones. But stopping the flood risks from rising over the next century means expensive investment in flood defences, raising runways, or even moving airports. It could cost up to $57bn (Â£42bn) according to the Newcastle report. Such costs are ultimately likely to be met by taxpayers or by travellers.

The aviation industry is often criticised for its own contribution to climate change emissions. It faces a forbidding challenge in attempting to meet a target of being carbon neutral by 2050, while also accommodating growth that is expected to see the global aircraft fleet double in size over the next two decades.

The overlooked factor driving the childhood obesity crisis

The race to stop England running out of water

How a new era of heatwaves could shake up the school calendar

Yet it is clear the impacts of climate change will also be felt by airlines, airports and inevitably by passengers -

"I think the best we can do is mitigate", says Andrew Charlton.

"We need to make sure we have enough space at air terminals for people whose flights are being disrupted and delayed, that we have enough hotel rooms and parking space and so on."

But ultimately, he says, "disagreeable moments are going to happen more".

Passengers, he thinks, will have to accept it as a fact of flying life.

#236. European stocks open lower after weak Wall Street finish, computer outages
