#160. What will the energy cap changes mean for my bills?

Energy bills are to soar in January, as prices governed by regulator Ofgem's cap rise at the coldest time of the year.

The cap affects millions of people in England, Scotland and Wales, and reflects higher wholesale gas costs which have been pushed up by the US-Israel war with Iran.

It means people on variable tariffs should give an accurate meter reading, ensure they are receiving all the support to which they are entitled, and take care over energy use.

The energy cap fixes the maximum amount which customers on standard variable tariffs can be charged for each unit of gas and electricity used.

It covers more than 20 million households in England, Wales and Scotland and is set by the regulator Ofgem every three months, external.

It affects those paying by direct debit, by standard credit (they pay when they get a bill), or prepayment meters in slightly different ways.

Most of the increase in the energy price cap has, and will be, driven by higher gas prices.

The government's decision to temporarily remove VAT from domestic electricity bills has cut about Â£45 a year from a typical bill.

Meanwhile, millions of households will receive a Â£100 automatic payment to help cover winter costs.

Customers' actual bills depend on the amount of energy they use and how they pay for it.

From January, for those on variable tariffs:

For electricity, average unit prices will go up on 1 January from 26.32p per kilowatt-hour to 32.69p

Gas will increase from 7.97p per kilowatt-hour to 10.96p, higher than in the wake of the invasion of Ukraine

Daily standing charges will be 54.77p for electricity and 29.65p for gas

The roughly 11 million households on fixed tariffs will not see their prices change until the end of their fixed term.

Ofgem regulates the energy market in England, Scotland and Wales. Northern Ireland has a separate system.

As well as how much energy you use and how you pay for it, where you live, the type of property you have, how energy efficient it is, how many people live there, and the weather all make a difference.

The Ofgem cap is illustrated using "typical" household energy use in a year with a single bill for gas and electricity settled by direct debit.

The vast majority of people pay their bill this way to help spread payments across the year.

In July, Ofgem reduced what it believes to be a "typical" level of energy use, because many homes have cut back due to high prices in recent years while energy efficiency has improved.

Its new estimate is 9,500 kWh of gas and 2,500 kWh of electricity a year.

Submitting a meter reading when the cap changes means you are not charged for estimated usage at the wrong rate.

This is especially important when prices go up.

Customers with working smart meters do not need to submit a reading as their bill is calculated automatically.

Energy bills will soar - here's how you can ease the pain

Ofgem also controls standing charges, which are a fixed daily fee to cover the costs of connecting households to gas and electricity supplies. These vary slightly by region, external and payment method.

Between 1 October and 31 December 2026, average standing charges for direct debit customers are 54.83p a day for electricity and 29.68p a day for gas.

From January, daily standing charges will be 54.77p for electricity and 29.65p for gas.

The electricity standing charge is lower than the amount charged over the previous three months, while the gas charge is largely unchanged.

Campaigners have long argued that standing charges are unfair because they make up a bigger proportion of the bill for low-energy households. Others say it just shifts the cost from one part of the bill to another rather than cutting it.

The government has announced plans to remove gas standing charges, although no details have been published on how that will happen

Fixed deals provide certainty for a set period â often a year, or longer â but if energy prices drop while you are on the deal, you could be stuck at a higher price.

You may also have to pay a penalty to leave a fixed deal early if you change your mind.

Experts recommend checking whole-of-market energy price comparison sites to help find the best deal and studying the details of exit fees.

Since 1 April, energy bill charges related to the low-income household insulation scheme - called the Energy Company Obligation - have been scrapped, and for three years, renewable energy projects will be 75%-funded by general taxation instead of a levy on energy bills.

Nearly everyone in England, Wales and Scotland will benefit from this cut, although the amounts will vary between households.

However, the cost of maintaining and strengthening energy network infrastructure like power lines, cables and gas pipes is rising.

In December 2025, Ofgem approved a Â£28bn investment to improve the electricity and gas grids in Great Britain to strengthen energy supplies and protect customers against volatile energy prices. It will also reduce Britain's dependence on gas.

Customers will pay part of the cost of the upgrade, through an additional Â£108 added to energy bills by 2031.

These charges started to appear from April 2026, adding about Â£6 a month to the bill for a typical household covered by the energy cap.

High electricity bills targeted in planned shakeup to energy pricing

Households could get free electricity for doing washing on sunny weekends

Suppliers must offer customers affordable payment plans or repayment holidays if they are struggling to pay their bills. Most also offer hardship grants.

Since the Russian invasion of Ukraine in 2022, bills have gone up by about 70% compared with the pre-crisis norm, and since then unpaid bills and charges have shot up.

Vanessa Northam, director at debt charity StepChange, has advice for people who may be struggling:

Take stock of all household money coming in and going out.

Look closely at how much energy you are using, and be alert to estimated bills, which might prove to be inaccurate.

Tell your supplier if you think you are going to struggle.

It is often best to try to talk to someone who can fully understand your situation.

Energy UK has a list of support schemes., external

Heating oil is not covered by the energy cap. About 1.5 million UK households use heating oil and they have already seen a sharp increase in bills since the Middle East conflict triggered a jump in oil prices. Some users have seen costs more than double.

The issue is particularly acute in Northern Ireland, where about 500,000 homes use heating all, almost two-thirds of all households.

In March, the government announced a Â£53m support package to help low-income households in rural communities who use heating oil.

Starmer announces Â£53m support to help with heating oil costs

How are rising costs caused by the Iran war affecting your quality of life or business?

#157. 'I only use the washing machine on a Sunday' - how we're keeping our energy bills down

Domestic energy prices will soar by 26% in January under the regulator Ofgem's new price cap, pushing up bills for millions of households across England, Scotland and Wales.

With household budgets already under pressure, finding ways to reduce gas and electricity costs has become increasingly important. But how much difference can simple changes really make?

The BBC spoke to three people about what they're doing to keep their energy costs down.

Kelly Anne Harvey uses electric blankets and portable oil radiators in some rooms to save on the central heating

Like many people, Kelly Anne Harvey, a building surveyor from Cleveleys near Blackpool, is worried about her energy bill going up next year.

The 44-year-old, who is married with a five-year-old son, has a combined household income of Â£60,000.

She currently pays about Â£154 a month for gas and electricity on a standard variable rate with Octopus, but is looking for a fixed-rate deal to offset the rise.

The family fell into arrears on their energy bills a few years ago and Kelly says she won't let it happen again.

To control her costs she pays by direct debit, which spreads her payments and is typically Â£138 cheaper a year than paying on receipt of a bill.

The family uses electric blankets and portable oil radiators in some rooms so they don't have to turn the central heating on as often.

They also take part in Octopus's energy saving scheme, which rewards customers who use less electricity at peak times, such as early evenings, with a free hour of energy at the weekend.

"On Sunday we'll turn on the dishwasher, tumble drier, washing machine and maybe even charge our electric cars," says Kelly.

Stacey says she keeps the curtains closed "to keep the heat in" and her daughters wear thermal clothing

Stacey, 40, a single mother from Bristol, says dealing with rising energy costs has felt like a "battle" recently and she's worried about 2027.

The office worker, whose daughters are aged 10 and 12, is on a fixed-term tracker tariff with E.On Next, which charges rates set below the quarterly Ofgem price cap. She expects to pay Â£1,800 for energy this year, up Â£400 on last year.

Stacey, who is part of the Changing Realities project at Glasgow University, which documents life for people on low incomes, has tried a number of things to control her energy costs.

She's turned her central heating down from 22C to 19.5C, bought electric blankets and pays her bills by direct debit.

She also leaves her curtains shut to "keep the heat in", while her daughters wear thermal clothing if it gets chilly in the house. The family is also using less hot water.

"I've got girls and they're growing up and they want to spend loads of time in the shower [but] it's just not possible."

Energy bills to rise by 26% from January after gas prices surge

Three reasons why your energy bill is so high

Will I get the Â£100 energy payment and when will I receive it?

Around 1.5 million households in the UK rely on heating oil for heating and hot water, and their costs have already soared in the past year due to disruption in global oil markets.

"When I first moved here two years ago you could get a litre of heating oil for 50-60p," says Bradley Knowles, 32, who lives in a barn conversion with his husband, mother and sister in rural Lincolnshire.

"At the height of the Iran war it went up to Â£1.20 or Â£1.30 and now it's stabilised at around Â£1."

To cut costs, the finance worker orders oil in bulk, buying 1,000-1,500 litres, preferably in the summer months when prices are lower. He also tries to order a few weeks in advance to avoid expensive next-day deliveries.

Bradley uses comparison sites to see which local suppliers offer the cheapest oil and puts money aside each month so he can always afford his orders.

Oil-powered boilers may take time to heat up, but Bradley advises against leaving them on all day as that can be very expensive.

Instead, he uses an electric tank to store his warm water and tops up the heating in parts of the property with electric radiators.

#154. Energy bills will soar - here's how you can ease the pain

Keeping yourself warm by wearing thick clothes, using blankets and having hot drinks can help reduce heating bills

Energy bills will soar by 26% in the new year as the war in Iran pushes up wholesale prices.

Typical household bills will rise by Â£37 per month as Ofgem's latest price cap comes into force from January to March, but experts say it is possible to keep rising bills in check.

Here are four areas to consider ahead of the increase.

Ofgem's price cap, as well as suppliers' fixed deals, set a limit on the price of each unit of energy, not the total bill.

So, you pay for what you use. Millions of people have cut back on their energy consumption during years of relatively high prices.

With the unit price of gas, in particular, set to soar, experts say you should take a fresh look at your energy use and renew good habits.

turning down the flow temperature on a combi boiler - usually indicated by a radiator icon, to make it operate more efficiently

turning off the pre-heat facility on some combi boilers, which would have kept some water heated up when not in use

turning down the thermostat one degree, although 18C is the recommended minimum

lowering radiator valves outside of the living room

These are small changes but - alongside washing clothes at 30C, blocking draughts and batch cooking - they can reduce energy use and bills.

Ofgem says about five million people still pay their bill when it arrives every three months, rather than receive a monthly bill often initially based on an estimate.

Taking regular meter readings, or having a smart meter, while paying by monthly direct debit solves that problem.

Paying by direct debit costs significantly less than by so-called standard credit - typically Â£138 cheaper a year.

Customers on prepayment schemes - usually people on low incomes struggling to pay bills - used to pay a higher rate than others, but now they pay slightly less than the capped direct debit variable rate.

Various deals may rely on paying by direct debit or having a smart meter.

Moving to a fixed energy tariff means the price you are charged per unit of gas or electricity does not change every three months, although the total bill will still vary depending on how much energy you use.

The trouble is that it is very difficult to work out whether a fixed deal is a good deal at the moment, for those renewing fixed deals or looking for one for the first time.

Some fixed deals are available at unit prices below the next forecast price cap level. But should energy prices fall sharply then a longer-term fix might not seem such good value compared with the price cap.

So consumer group Which? also recommends paying close attention to any exit fees when picking a fixed deal. If variable prices fall below your fixed rate and you want to get out of it before the end of your agreement period, you may have to pay a hefty sum.

Before you fix, consumer website MoneySavingExpert advises you look at all the deals available, not just what your own supplier is offering.

The government will give a Â£100 payment to millions of homes to help cover winter energy costs. It will go to homes in council tax bands A to D in England, with the equivalent expected in Scotland and Wales.

There are also a range of other forms of support specifically for people on low incomes.

This winter anyone on means-tested benefits will get Â£150 off their electricity bill.

Your supplier will apply the discount automatically unless you are in Scotland, where you will need to contact your energy company to ask for the help.

Some people will also be eligible for a Cold Weather Payment, external and most pensioners will receive the winter fuel payment.

Beyond that, most energy suppliers offer hardship grants for people struggling to pay their bills and the government's Fuel Direct Scheme, external can help you repay energy debt.

You can get more advice from Ofgem, external, Citizens Advice, external, or your own energy supplier. Trade body Energy UK has a list of support schemes, external.

People on low incomes may also be eligible for grants to help with insulating their homes, and there are local schemes offering grants for solar panels, heat pumps and the like.

But if you are hunting around for grants, watch out for scams pretending to be official money-off schemes.

How is the cost of energy affecting your quality of life? What questions do you have?

#151. Trump's accounts traded Nvidia shares 44 times this year, records show

Trump's portfolio traded Nvidia shares 44 times this year

Donald Trump's investment portfolios have bought or sold shares in chipmaker Nvidia at least 44 times this year, a review of the US president's financial disclosures shows.

Trump's most recent public declarations of his investment trading show that between January and September the portfolios purchased between $5.2m and $17m (Â£4m and Â£13m) in Nvidia stock - and sold between $4.4m and $12m. His portfolios held between $12.5m and $58m of Nvidia shares at the end of 2025.

Financial disclosures to the Office of Government Ethics report a low and high range of transaction values, so a more precise analysis of how much Nvidia stock was traded is not possible.

The period of recent turmoil at Nvidia and volatility in its share price is not covered by the disclosures. In the last two weeks, co-founder and chief executive Jensen Huang resigned and then Trump played a significant part in his reinstatement.

The White House has said that Trump's stock and bond portfolio is independently managed by third parties and neither he nor his family "has any ability to direct, influence or provide input regarding how the portfolio is invested or when investments are bought or sold".

Huang resigned on 12 November after reports of extramarital affairs, including with one woman who has alleged ties to Chinese intelligence. Nvidia's share price dived following news of his resignation.

But on Monday Nvidia's board announced the 63-year-old was being reinstated as CEO.

Trump told US media he had "made the deal" to "convince" the tech giant's board to bring Huang back. The board said it had "received input from senior levels of the United States government" that he posed "no national security risk".

Nvidia's share price surged 31% on Monday following Huang's return. About $1tn (Â£763bn) was added to Nvidia's market value, but the company's shares are still trading below their value before reports of Huang's alleged affairs began to circulate.

However, concerns remain among both Republican and Democratic politicians about Huang's actions and Nvidia's compliance with US controls over the export of advanced computer chips to China, as well as Trump's involvement in getting him reappointed as CEO.

Nvidia's chip technology and investments have been critical to the growth of the artificial intelligence (AI) industry, leading to it becoming one of the world's most valuable companies.

The firm now represents more than 6% of the S&P 500 share index, according to State Street financial services. This makes it the second most valuable company on the index after Apple, whose shares Trump's accounts also frequently trade.

Nvidia also ranks highly in many global indices, meaning that investment accounts held around the world, including pensions, are likely to include its shares.

Our analysis of Trump's Nvidia trades does not include other investment products held by him which may include its shares.

Trump made at least $2.2bn (Â£1.7bn) from a variety of investments and commercial activities, according to the financial disclosure report for the first year of Trump's second term.

Barbara Perry, a presidential historian at the University of Virginia's Miller Center, told the BBC there is "no precedent for this," adding: "It's beyond anything we've ever seen in the presidency."

#149. Planes, trains and automobiles: The best ways to beat travel fatigue—so you can come home feeling rested and fulfilled

Five-hour flights. Eleven-hour road trips. Fifteen-hour train rides. All potentially exhausting options for the annual tradition of convening around the dinner table with loved ones on Thanksgiving.

An estimated 81.5 million Americans are projected to travel, or have already traveled, at least 50 miles from their homes between Nov. 24 and Nov. 30, according to the American Automobile Association. And their mode of transportation is shifting: Fewer people are flying or driving this year compared to 2025, and more are traveling by buses, trains and cruises, AAA data shows.

High gas prices and soaring plane ticket costs may factor into the shift. As of Wednesday evening, fuel prices are roughly 17% higher than they were on the same date in 2025, according to AAA data. Meanwhile, "months of elevated jet fuel costs due to the conflict in Iran have contributed to Thanksgiving and Christmas airfares reaching 10-year highs," according to fare-tracking platform Hopper.

The alternate modes of transit may open travelers up to new experiences — and new forms of travel fatigue to keep you from returning home feeling rested and fulfilled. You're probably already familiar with the exhaustion you might get from a day of plane travel, for example. In airplanes, "there can be lower humidity, maybe lower oxygen levels at cabin pressure. Sometimes people report feeling more dehydrated or drained. You're adjusting to that change in altitude," says Alaina Tiani, a Cleveland Clinic behavioral sleep medicine psychologist.

But even waiting in places like train stations or cruise ports can feel draining due to sheer sensory overload, Tiani says. And then, depending on your mode of travel, you may still need to deal with cramped seating and limited ability to get up and walk around or drink some water and eat a snack.

Once you arrive at your destination, managing responsibilities like remembering where you put your hotel key and deciding what to eat and how to structure your day can also take a mental toll, says travel writer Shannon O'Donnell, who's written about managing travel fatigue. "All of that actually requires a lot more thinking and planning for your brain," she says, adding: "People just don't understand how those add up and why a relaxing vacation suddenly has them feeling wrecked."

The consequences can sneak up on you, O'Donnell notes. Travel fatigue can "present as maybe a lack of interest in something that you might usually find fascinating," she says. Instead of looking out the window on a plane ride or playing car games with your kids on a road trip, you might disengage and appear apathetic to the people around you.

You might stand a better chance of a fulfilling, restful holiday if you prepare for your travel fatigue. Here, Tiani and O'Donnell share their advice for how to do it:

#147. Retail turnarounds are taking hold just in time for the holiday season

Once-struggling retailers are entering the holiday quarter with improved performance and sales momentum as turnaround efforts begin to resonate with shoppers.

A handful of earnings reports on Tuesday pointed to a mix of factors behind the strong results, including a resilient consumer, stronger execution and demand for the right products at the right price.

Kohl's reported comparable sales growth of 1.2% for the period ended Oct. 31 and raised its full-year outlook as CEO Michael Bender said customers "have remained resilient" but are "migrating to value with their purchases."

He tied improvement to the company's work on product assortment, store experience and value.

Best Buy also on Tuesday posted its highest comparable sales growth in five years, at 6.1%, which prompted the electronics retailer to raise its full-year outlook.

"We have a tremendous amount of momentum," CEO Jason Bonfig told CNBC in an interview. "The fact that we saw pretty much broad-based growth across almost all of our categories is a lot of momentum that we're very excited about."

The company also credited execution, market share gains and a compelling product cycle alongside growing customer demand.

At Dick's Sporting Goods, quarterly comparable sales rose 2.6% at the core business and 0.5% at Foot Locker. CEO Lauren Hobart said "all income demographics grew" and the company "didn't see trade-down from best to better or better to good."

She similarly pointed to new products and market share gains within the category as contributing to the strong quarter. She also highlighted continued improvement at Foot Locker.

All three companies have been undergoing efforts to boost sales and get the right products on shelves to meet customer demand.

Target, likewise undergoing revitalization efforts, last week reported comparable sales growth of 5.6% in the third quarter, fueled by a 5% increase in comparable traffic. The retailer raised its full-year outlook.

CEO Michael Fiddelke at the time called the traffic gain "a healthy sign that guests are responding to our changes and choosing Target more often."

Even Gap CEO Richard Dickson told CNBC the company has moved from "rebuild mode" to "turnaround mode" at its Old Navy banner, a sign of progress, albeit not a broad-based portfolio recovery.

Michael Baker, a retail analyst at D.A. Davidson, told CNBC he sees both a resilient consumer and company-specific improvements as driving sector strength.

"Retail sales have been more resilient than most think," Baker said. His analysis showed retail sales, excluding autos and gas, rose 5.1% in the third quarter on a nonseasonally adjusted basis — the strongest quarter since the pandemic.

But he cautioned that growth appears to be coming more from transaction size than traffic, with inflation, promotions and higher units per transaction driving up spend.

Dollar Tree, which reported better-than-expected earnings on Tuesday morning, posted a 3.8% increase to traffic, accelerating from 0.4% growth in the prior quarter, suggesting more shoppers may be bargain hunting. Comparable sales at the retailer increased 4.6% during the period.

"Consumers are under pressure, and they are increasingly selective about where and how they spend," said Dollar Tree CEO Michael Creedon on the company's earnings call. "When under pressure, shoppers prioritize value and buy closer to need."

Comparable sales of everyday essentials rose 8.9% at Dollar Tree stores in the third quarter, while sales of nonessential items were essentially flat, the company said.

— CNBC's Laya Neelakandan contributed to this report.

#146. Energy bills to rise by 26% from January after gas prices surge

Watch: Martin Lewis' tips on staying warm this winter

Energy bills will soar by 26% for millions of households in January after a surge in gas prices sparked by the Iran war.

A typical bill will increase by about Â£37 a month in the first three months of the new year, although the price will depend on how much energy a household uses, so it could be closer to Â£60 extra a month in winter.

A household using a typical amount of energy in England, Scotland and Wales and paying by direct debit, would see their annual bill rise from Â£1,723 to Â£2,166 if the increase was sustained for a year, said regulator Ofgem.

The government will pay millions of households Â£100 from 4 January to help cover winter costs on top of an existing VAT cut on electricity.

Charities and suppliers expect debt to rise and a wave of people to need emergency support.

Ofgem said the rise had been driven entirely by higher wholesale costs faced by suppliers owing, primarily, to the Iran war. Wholesale gas prices, paid by suppliers, were up 33% in the last three months, the period used to set the January to March cap. They have more than doubled since late 2025.

The warm home discount will take Â£150 off bills for six million households on benefits this winter. There have been calls for this support, funded through everyone's bill, to be extended.

"We are staring down the barrel of a brutal winter," said Dame Clare Moriarty, chief executive of Citizens Advice.

Neil Kenward, Ofgem's director general for markets, said it wrote to energy suppliers "reminding them of their responsibilities" to customers this winter. They have been told to identify people who need help early, make support easy to access and ensure repayment plans are affordable and sustainable.

Campaign Groups Friends of the Earth and Global Witness said major oil and energy companies are making huge profits because of spiralling oil costs, hurting households.

Energy UK, which represents suppliers, said the firms which send your bill, have profits capped on a percentage basis and do not see the same benefit as international energy generating companies. However, higher bills will mean higher profits in nominal cash terms.

The increase is the biggest since Russia's invasion of Ukraine drove up prices. It comes at a tough time of year, when the weather is at its coldest.

People on prepayment meters will be hit as their spending comes when energy is needed, rather than the cost being spread over the year as with direct debits.

The cap sets a maximum price for each unit of energy, not the total bill. Those on fixed deals are unaffected until the end of their term, but for those on variable tariffs it means:

For electricity, average unit prices will go up on 1 January from 26.32p per kilowatt-hour to 32.69p. The electricity price is often pegged to gas, owing to gas-fired generation.

Gas will increase from 7.97p per kilowatt-hour to 10.96p, higher than in the wake of the invasion of Ukraine.

Daily standing charges will be 54.77p for electricity and 29.65p for gas.

The cap is set every three months, based on the costs faced by suppliers as well as the cost of running the energy system.

It is based on a three-month period that ends about six weeks before the new cap comes into force. A fall in international wholesale prices in recent days will not be reflected in January's cap, but it will be in April's.

Energy Secretary Miatta Fahnbulleh said people will be "deeply concerned" by another rise in energy bills.

"For too long, British families have been paying the price for conflicts overseas and volatile fossil fuel markets," she said.

Andrew Bowie, shadow energy secretary, said: "It is an outrage that, as bills soar, Labour are not only piling green levies and taxes onto bills but signing us up to an EU scheme that risks pushing them even higher.

Stacey says she keeps the curtains closed "to keep the heat in" and her daughters wear thermal clothing

Stacey, 40, a single mother from Bristol, said dealing with rising energy costs has felt like a "battle" recently and she's worried about 2027.

She's turned her central heating down from 22C to 19.5C, bought electric blankets and pays her bills by direct debit.

She also leaves her curtains shut to "keep the heat in", while her daughters wear thermal clothing if it gets chilly in the house.

'I only use the washing machine on a Sunday' - how we're keeping our energy bills down

Energy bills will soar - here's how you can ease the pain

Will I get the Â£100 energy payment and when will I receive it?

Up to 20 million households in England will get a Â£100 payment from 4 January to help with their energy bills this winter, with equivalent payments in Scotland and Wales.

Citizens Advice said the money will disappear quickly, particularly for those on prepayment meters, leading to a wave of requests for emergency help.

Energy UK said the funding was poorly targeted and insufficient to stem rising levels of customer debt.

The government said councils had a Â£100m discretionary fund to support vulnerable households which fell outside of the main scheme.

#144. US stocks fall further as fears over AI intensify

Big technology firms connected to AI continued to see share prices fall on Wednesday, with stock in Nvidia and Anthropic dropping sharply in early trading.

The surge in Nvidia shares after boss Jensen Huang was reinstated was temporary, and the general stock sell-off continued, with all three main US stock market indices down by closing time. The tech-dominated Nasdaq closed 0.6% lower.

The declines came amid concerns over the level and sustainability of AI investment, which has rattled investors in recent weeks.

Shares in other major tech firms, including Amazon, Tesla and Microsoft closed the day lower, though Meta and SpaceX were in positive territory by the end of the trading day.

Nvidia's share price was down 8% in early trading on Wednesday, extending Tuesday's slide as the boost from Huang's return eased. At close, it ended up 3.4% lower.

The resignation and subsequent reinstatement of Nvidia's CEO after revelations of his extramarital affairs has seen the market value of the world's premier AI chipmaker fluctuate wildly.

Given Nvidia's position as both supplier and financier of many other firms involved in the AI rollout, its rocky fortnight has had a wider impact.

The US government also announced a raft of new tariffs on countries and trading partners on Wednesday as part of President Donald Trump's flagship trade policy.

The value of big tech companies have sheltered the US economy from global economic shocks, including tariffs.

But share prices in recent weeks have been volatile in response to the US midterm elections and other events in the AI industry.

Faisal Islam: Fears over the AI boom haven't gone away

Investors have taken fright at Democrat gains in the US midterm elections, with many having campaigned for tougher regulation of AI firms and even a moratorium on the development of new data centres.

Questions also remain about whether the huge sums being invested in AI will eventually pay off.

"It seems that these companies are trying to one-up each other on the amount of money that they're spending, but you have to show that it's worth it, and that's where Wall Street is saying I don't know," said Todd Schoenberger, chief investment officer at CrossCheck Management.

Despite concerns, investment in AI is expected to exceed $1 trillion in 2026, according to investment bank Goldman Sachs, which said the technology will remain the "dominant driver of business investment growth" next year.

Shares in Anthropic continued to trade below the $83 they debuted at last week, while Amazon shares fell by just over 3%.

The declines come ahead of the US stock market closing for Thanksgiving on Thursday before opening for a shortened session on Friday.

#142. Trump, NATO chief caution Russia as attacks on Ukraine’s energy infrastructure intensify

U.S. President Donald Trump and NATO chief Mark Rutte issued a joint statement on Thursday urging Russia not to take any actions, intentionally or otherwise, that would further expand the Ukraine war.

The statement comes as Ukraine's allies sound the alarm over Russia's growing threat to European security and just one week after Trump's historic trilateral meeting with Russian President Vladimir Putin and Chinese President Xi Jinping in Asia.

Trump and Rutte described their meeting at the Mar-a-Lago club in Palm Beach, Florida on Wednesday as "very impactful," with Russia's nearly five-year-long war in Ukraine and the Iran war among the key topics of conversation.

"We also discussed the importance of Russia avoiding any actions that intentionally, or unintentionally, expand the war, and the importance of obtaining a peaceful resolution to the war as rapidly as possible," Trump and Rutte said in their statement.

Moreover, the two discussed the importance of further increasing defense spending as well as investment in the defense industrial base in both the U.S. and NATO countries, according to the statement.

Trump also said in a post on Truth Social Thursday that he "briefed NATO on the tremendous progress" being made in growing defense production, noting that the focus will be on building Patriot air defense systems, THAAD systems and Tomahawk cruise missiles, as well as "many new Weapons, both Offensive and Defensive, that have not yet been viewed by the Public, or other Countries."

"There has never been a time when the United States has produced more Military Equipment or Munitions than now. Our 'cupboard' will soon be full and, when it is, our Allies and Partners throughout the World will be benefitting greatly," he said in the post.

Trump's meeting with Xi and Putin, in which he described the Russian president as his "friend," sparked alarm among European lawmakers about Washington's commitment to transatlantic security.

NATO's Rutte, a seasoned diplomat known as a consensus builder during his nearly 14 years as Dutch prime minister, has repeatedly sought to de-escalate tensions in the alliance and frequently used flattery to win over the U.S. president.

While some have questioned whether this approach has delivered any tangible benefits, Trump has previously described Rutte as a "great leader" and NATO's "biggest asset."

Russia has stepped up its attacks on Ukraine's energy infrastructure in recent weeks, with further attacks on the central Ukrainian city of Kryvyi Rih overnight disrupting power supplies and bringing municipal electric public transport to a standstill, according to Oleksandr Vilkul, head of Kryvyi ​Rih's defense council.

Ukrainian officials on Wednesday said Russian attacks on the country's power sector had damaged energy infrastructure and caused power outages in five regions as cold winter weather sets in.

German Chancellor Friedrich Merz on Wednesday accused the Kremlin of "escalating" threats toward European security, citing actions in the Baltic region, Poland and on the Black Sea, and adding that "Russia knows exactly what it wants. And Russia wants more than Ukraine."

A spokesperson for Russia's Foreign Ministry did not immediately respond to a CNBC request for comment on Thursday morning. The Kremlin has repeatedly denied seeking military confrontation with NATO countries.

#141. Musk's plan to send a million SpaceX data satellites into space approved

SpaceX is an aerospace company that builds rockets and spacecraft, as well as running satellite services

US regulators have given SpaceX the go-ahead to launch a million satellites into Earth's orbit to help build data centres in space - the first approval of its kind.

The company, which is owned by Elon Musk, wants to use sunlight to power satellites that will process data for AI and send the results back to Earth.

The Federal Communications Commission (FCC) said on Wednesday that the project, known as Starmind, would "support America's booming super intelligence economy".

However, rival firm Amazon has criticised it as a "lofty ambition rather than a real plan", while astronomers in the US said the move would "significantly undermine" the public's ability to study and observe the night sky.

There is increasing opposition to data centres on earth, including in the US where experts say they were a factor in the Republicans big losses in the midterm elections.

SpaceX believes the "orbital data centres" are the most cost and energy-efficient way to meet the growing demand for artificial intelligence (AI) computing power that is beyond "terrestrial capabilities".

FCC Chairman Brendan Carr said in a statement that the project "opens a bold new frontier for the computing infrastructure needed to power America's leadership and growth".

"This is the kind of American ingenuity that can unite our strengths in space and advanced technology, reinforce our economic leadership, and expand prosperity here at home," Carr said.

Responding to the announcement, Musk wrote on X: "Much appreciated. This will be a great asset to America and the world."

Did a data centre revolt affect the US midterm election results?

Why Canada is embracing AI data centres while the US pushes back

Under the FCC licence, deployment of the satellites must begin no later than November 2033. SpaceX will be required to have deployed 10% by November 2035, rising to 50% by November 2038.

The company has already said it wants to launch its first Starmind satellite next year.

It is unclear whether a million satellites will indeed be launched or whether SpaceX has sought approval for a higher number than it will use.

Like SpaceX's current Starlink network, which provides high-speed internet via more than 10,000 satellites, Starmind's satellites will operate in low-Earth orbit at altitudes ranging between 500-2,000km (310-1,242 miles).

Starmind's satellites will communicate with Starlink's and then relay the data to Earth.

The plans to put data centres in orbit and on the Moon

Speaking to the Today programme, on BBC Radio 4, Prof Sir Martin Sweeting, founder and executive chairman of Surrey Satellite Technology Ltd, said while the idea of data centres in space seemed "technically feasible", whether it "makes sense at scale yet" still needed to be analysed.

Being able to get the data from the network of satellites, he added, would depend on being able to use laser links from the ground to them.

"That has not yet been perfected to the level at which it would be able to support the huge amounts of data that are likely to be transferred backwards and forwards," he said.

Meanwhile, the American Astronomical Society (AAS) said it was "disheartened and dismayed" by the decision.

It said the proposed constellation would "significantly undermine decades of federal investment in astronomical facilities" as well as "the ability of astronomers and the public to enjoy and study the night sky".

It noted that the constellation "involves larger (and therefore brighter) and more numerous objects", meaning the satellites would "be visible as extremely bright objects for longer periods in the night sky".

Jan Hasselman, a senior attorney at the non-profit group Earthjustice, said in a statement: "Drastically expanding satellites in space has a direct impact on people's everyday lives as well as the future of our planet."

The BBC visited Arizona before the US midterm elections to understand America's data-centre boom

The orbiting data centre concept is also being explored by other firms - including Google and Blue Origin - and is being pitched as a greener alternative to traditional centres, which require enormous amounts of power to run and water for cooling.

The ever-expanding use of AI computing has seen a massive increase in the amount of data that needs to be stored and processed around the world.

New facilities continue to be built but it is becoming difficult to find places to put them. Data centres are large and sprawling, use enormous amounts of power and water for cooling, and increasingly local people don't want them built nearby.

An expert previously told the BBC that launching hardware into orbit remains expensive and that the infrastructure to protect, cool and power them can be complex - while a growing quantity of space debris puts the physical hardware at risk.

Separately, another cautioned that the growing number of low-orbit craft increases the possibility of crashes between objects - which could damage machines or send materials falling back to earth.

Starlink has been accused of creating congestion in space, something Musk denies. He has also rejected claims his satellites have been taking up too much room and crowding out competitors.

#138. US announces new tariffs on host of countries including China

Watch: What we know about Trump's Thanksgiving tariffs

The US has announced a fresh raft of tariffs as President Donald Trump pushes ahead with his signature trade policy.

The new taxes charged on imports to America are set to hit many of the country's biggest trading partners - China, the European Union and 12 other jurisdictions, including Japan, South Korea and Taiwan.

The White House began investigating the trade practices of other countries in March after the US Supreme Court ruled Trump's previously announced Liberation Day tariffs were illegal.

China is facing the highest tariff rate of up to 40%, followed by Vietnam at up to 35%, with others facing either 20% or 25%.

The US Trade Representative (USTR) announced the proposed tariffs together with extensive reports of how each jurisdiction's trade practices purportedly impact American commerce.

The new duties are not expected to take effect until next year and have to go through rounds of public consultation and, potentially, hearings.

Tariffs are taxes levied on imported goods. The amount is typically calculated as a percentage of a good's value and paid to the government by businesses importing products.

Some of these rates will be effectively reduced because of caps on tariffs Trump has agreed to. Individual countries also face different levies.

The proposed tariffs will affect goods from China, the European Union, Singapore, Switzerland, Indonesia, Malaysia, Cambodia, Thailand, Korea, Vietnam, Taiwan, Bangladesh, Japan, and India.

The USTR's Jamieson Greer said the latest tariffs issued would "level the playing field for American workers and chart a new, more sustainable course for the world economy".

He said that many of America's trading partners had "for decades" adopted industrial strategies which included "targeting manufacturing, wage and labor suppression".

They also employ "other market distorting practices that tilt the trade balance in their favour while eroding America's manufacturing base", Greer said.

But economists have warned tariffs can fuel inflation and push prices up for consumers if businesses pass on higher costs, as well as dampen global trade.

Trump has repeatedly threatened or imposed tariffs during his second term, arguing they will boost US manufacturing and create jobs.

Despite a poor result for the Republican party in the midterm elections, the president has continued to press on with his flagship trade policy.

Trump is also seeking to cut the US trade deficit - the gap between the value of goods it buys from other countries and those it sells to them.

Trump's so-called Liberation Day tariffs were struck down by the Supreme Court earlier this year

These new levies are build on a "stronger legal foundation" under US law compared with Trump's April 2025 Liberation Day tariffs, Singapore Management University economics lecturer Chang Pao-Li said.

Trump previously invoked emergency presidential powers to impose tariffs that have since been struck down, while his latest approach relies on the USTR's authority to impose tariffs to address certain foreign trade practices, she said.

The levies are still likely to face legal challenges, but there has been a "historical precedent" for such tariffs remaining in force, Chang said.

Many tariffs imposed under Section 301 - a law that allows the US to probe and retaliate against foreign trade practices - during Trump's first term remained in effect during his successor Joe Biden's administration.

However, Stephen Brown, chief North America economist for Capital Economics, said the latest tariff announcement was "not as alarming as it looks", due to the taxes "essentially replacing" previously announced tariffs made in the summer.

"The increase in the effective tariff rate looks set to be a touch lower than we've been assuming for our forecasts, although the new tariffs will still provide a marginal boost to prices," he said in a note.

The additional tariff burden is likely to be small because a limited number of goods are subject to the new duties, Brown added.

Jurisdictions affected by the new wave of tariffs have voiced their disapproval over the Trump administration's move.

Washington has also proposed a 10% tariff on goods from Brazil, as part of a separate investigation over what it says are "harmful trade practices" undercutting American workers.

Brazilian authorities expressed their "outrage" at the announcement, saying that the move is aimed at pressuring the government to change regulations around issues like digital trade.

The South American nation faces duties on finished aircraft and sugar sold to the US.

"Brazil will not yield to such threats," President Luiz Inacio Lula da Silva's office said.

The European Commission rejected the new US tariffs, saying it would investigate the reasons for bringing them in. It pointed out that tariffs are limited to the 15% previously agreed.

The latest tariff rate is "not consistent" with the reality of bilateral economic-trade cooperation between the US and Vietnam, Vietnamese Deputy Prime Minister Bui Thanh Son said.

The Japanese government said the US tariffs were "regrettable", while the Singapore government said it disagrees with the White House's findings and that it is engaging the USTR to explore ways to reduce the tariff.

South Korea's trade minister said the proposed 15% tariff will be "more than a decent outcome" as it would fall in line with a previously agreed limit with Washington.

Last year, Seoul pledged $350bn (Â£267bn) in US investments in exchange for lower tariffs as part of a trade deal.

Trump tariffs on Asia: Which economies were hit hardest?

China's growth accelerates as AI boom helps boost exports

US trading partners have been "waiting for this shoe to drop" and have argued that they are being wrongly accused of producing too much, said the Asia Society Policy Institute's Wendy Cutler, a former senior US trade negotiator.

"Many believe that China, not them, should be the target for US action," she added.

Chinese Foreign Ministry spokesperson Guo Jiakun described the new tariffs as a form of "political manipulation", describing as "false" the US claim that China produces more than it can consume.

The tariff announcement is likely to increase tensions between the US and China, the world's two biggest economies, who remain locked in a trade dispute and the AI race.

The two countries have also been embroiled in a tit-for-tat tariffs war, which is currently on hold.

Despite last week's meeting between Trump and China's President Xi Jinping, the two sides made no announcement on the trade war truce, which is set to expire on 10 January.

Beijing has remained vague on how it will respond but for now seems "prepared to live with the new rates provided they don't exceed 20%", Cutler said.

#137. Global stocks rally as investors shrug off U.S. tariffs and AI jitters to 'fish for bargains'

Global stocks rallied on Thursday, staging a tentative recovery from an intense AI-driven sell-off and looked past a fresh set of tariffs unveiled by the White House on Wednesday.

U.S. markets will be closed Thursday for the Thanksgiving holiday and will have a shortened trading session on Friday. During Wednesday's regular session, the Dow gained 0.33%, the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite fell 0.62% and the S&P 500 shed 0.25%, with the major averages paring losses seen earlier in the trading day.

In Europe on Thursday, equities rose sharply. The pan-European Stoxx 600 was 1.6% higher by 12:20 p.m. in London (7:20 a.m. ET), with all sectors and major bourses in positive territory.

Germany's DAX led gains on a rise of 1.9%, while the Stoxx 600 Technology index jumped 2.5%. The regional tech index has lost around 12% so far this month, amid jitters around a potential AI bubble bursting and the ousting — and return — of Jensen Huang as Nvidia CEO.

German semiconductor firm Infineon was more than 4% higher in afternoon trading, as chip stocks notched a boost. ASMI and ASML each gained just over 3%, while Be Semiconductor added 3.5%.

Asia-Pacific markets also rose on Thursday, with regional tech stocks also rebounded sharply, led by Japanese chip-related companies.

South Korea's Kospi gained 0.29% to close at 4,711.21, while the small-cap Kosdaq index advanced 0.78% to 655.71 after the country's central bank hiked benchmark lending rates to a 2-year high in its final meeting of the year.

Japan's Nikkei 225 rose 0.86% to 57,499.16 while the Topix edged 0.11% higher to 3,750.4. Australia's S&P/ASX 200 gained 0.82% to 8,763.8.

Hong Kong's Hang Seng index was up 1.52% in its last hour of trade, while mainland China's CSI 300 edged higher marginally, closing at 3,900.55.

SoftBank Group surged more than 7%, while chip-testing equipment maker Advantest rose 4.57%. Lasertec and Disco gained , while Tokyo Electron climbed 0.59%

In South Korea, memory chip giants SK Hynix and Samsung Electronics advanced more than 2% and 0.35%, respectively. The gains followed a bruising session Wednesday amid renewed concerns over the sustainability of artificial intelligence spending.

Russ Mould, investment director at AJ Bell, labeled it a "solid day" for regional markets, suggesting investors were hunting for opportunities in the wake of an intense global sell-off.

"The rollercoaster stock market is back on an upwards path as a new round of Trump tariffs aren't as bad as feared and the US takes the day off to celebrate Thanksgiving," he said in a note on Thursday morning. "It was notable that investors cast their net wide for opportunities, fishing for bargains in the rubble following the tech sell-off and looking at other sectors in the quest for diversification."

But Mould warned investors not to be "too complacent."

"Oil prices heading higher is not good for efforts to tame inflation, neither is the prospect of retaliation by countries on the receiving end of new US tariffs," he said.

The Trump administration on Wednesday unveiled a fresh barrage of tariffs on more than a dozen trading partners, including China, the European Union and India.

Daniela Hathorn, senior financial market analyst at Capital.com, said in a Thursday note that the mood in markets was "cautious rather than uniformly negative."

"European markets are attempting to rebound after yesterday's decline, while U.S. cash markets are closed for Thanksgiving, limiting the chance to test whether Wall Street's late recovery can hold," she said. "The main focus now will be trying to determine how many further increases the Fed considers necessary in early 2027 and whether AI shares can withstand that rate path while investors continue to question the returns on heavy spending."

U.S. inflation data on Wednesday reinforced expectations of further monetary tightening by the Federal Reserve. The personal consumption expenditures price index rose 3.8% year on year in October, accelerating from 3.7% in September, while core inflation came in slightly above expectations at 3.3%.

Mark Haefele, chief investment officer at UBS Global Wealth Management, said in a note on Thursday morning that his team believes investors should maintain strategic allocations to equities.

But he advised market participants to "review current allocations and use market moves as an opportunity to rebalance, refocus, and strengthen portfolio resilience."

#134. What's gone wrong at Poundland?

Watch: Why has Poundland collapsed into administration?

The future of Poundland hangs in the balance after the discount retailer's collapse into administration.

Its owner, US investment firm Gordon Brothers, put it up for sale in September, but then rejected offers to keep the business afloat.

Poundland has a major presence on the High Street, with 600 stores in the UK and Ireland, where it trades under the Dealz brand.

Its collapse puts the jobs of its 11,000 employees at risk, as well as potentially thousands of others at firms that supply the retailer.

People have not stopped shopping at Poundland - quite the opposite.

Although the chain was struggling when it was sold by Pepco last year for Â£1, the business turned a corner over the year to September, going from a Â£68m loss to an Â£18.1m profit before interest, tax and certain accounting charges.

Going into the vital Christmas period, first-quarter like-for-like sales were up 7% and operational profits of Â£1.9m were up Â£1.2m on last year.

According to Julie Palmer, managing partner at insolvency specialist Begbies Traynor, "this isn't a story about shoppers abandoning Poundland".

She told the BBC the tills were "busier than they were a year ago", adding that on the face of it a turnaround seemed "well under way".

However, Gordon Brothers had been restricting Poundland's access to cash needed to restock stores and meet day-to-day expenses.

Poundland faced "a funding and ownership problem rather than a trading collapse", according to Palmer.

However, while Poundland boss Barry Williams claimed the business was making a "strong recovery", Gordon Brothers painted a different picture, saying the chain remained "challenged and vulnerable to any potential downturn in future trading".

It said it had provided significant funding to support the company's ability to trade since it acquired the business.

Poundland collapses putting 11,000 jobs at risk

Poundland workers 'in limbo' over firm's future

It has also made about Â£18m from a combination of debt interest payments and other fees and charges.

As well as being Poundland's owner, it is one of its lenders, and is owed loans, including a Â£22.5m shareholder loan it inherited when it bought the business for Â£1.

As the main creditor, Gordon Brothers would be first in line to get paid back if the business goes under - but its suppliers are not.

"When the owner is also the main lender, its first priority is recovering [loans]," says Palmer. "Sometimes the route that best protects the lender is different from the one that saves the most jobs, and that tension is why this has become such a political story."

Irish firm Martin Property Group, London-based holding company Xyra Group and German investment firm Zachert Private Equity are all also understood to have made offers for Poundland.

Gordon Brothers had also been weighing options to break Poundland up.

Its view was that ultimately breaking the retailer up would provide more security for employees in the long term, because any buyers of its store estate would also have to agree to keep the employees on.

Insiders who are critical of how Gordon Brothers has handled the Poundland sale accuse it of driving the business into the ground to make money through the insolvency process.

But the investment firm, which specialises in buying and restructuring distressed companies, has denied the claim.

Now that the company has entered administration, consultants at Alix Partners have been appointed to pursue a sale of the business and its assets.

There are various options on the table, which include securing a buyer for the business either in whole or piecemeal.

The prime minister has said he wants "every effort made to secure a deal that protects jobs and keeps as many shops open as possible".

In the immediate future, Poundland stores and distribution centres will continue operating as normal.

#133. Poundland collapses putting 11,000 jobs at risk

Watch: Why has Poundland collapsed into administration?

Poundland has collapsed into administration, putting 11,000 jobs at risk, after its owners failed to find a buyer to keep it in business.

The retailer's shops and distribution centres will remain open and continue to trade during the administration process with the business likely to be broken up and some of its stores sold off to rival retailers.

Poundland's US owners held talks with several bidders over a last-minute rescue deal, but no agreement was reached.

Poundland boss Barry Williams said in a note to staff that the board had "no alternative but to take this decision", but was making it with "sincere sadness and intense regret".

He said the US owners Gordon Brothers had removed "all borrowing facilities and access to our bank accounts" on 27 October, and adequate funds had not been released since then to keep the business solvent.

"Under these circumstances, it is impossible for the board of directors to see how Poundland can continue to operate as a going concern," he added.

Bally Auluk, national officer at the union Usdaw, which represents Poundland workers, told the BBC'S Wake Up to Money programme that Gordon Brothers' "intention was perfectly clear from the offset... to force the company into administration".

"It's such a devastating blow any time of year, but most certainly in the run up to Christmas for 11,000 colleagues, most of whom are our members," he said.

Gordon Brothers rejected Auluk's characterisation of the process and said it had "explored every avenue to secure a sale of the whole company".

Kelvin Blake, pictured at a protest in Walsall, has worked for Poundland for 25 years

Kelvin Blake, 62, who has worked for the firm for 25 years, and whose partner has been there 29 years, said he was feeling "down, depressed", and because of his age, feared he would not find a job elsewhere.

"We didn't want it, but it's come so now we've just got to face it and see what the future holds.

"My income's gone," he added. "Obviously, we'll be working until we're told to leave."

Prime Minister Andy Burnham said Poundland was "part of the fabric of high streets and communities up and down the country".

He said the news of it going into administration just weeks before Christmas would be "deeply concerning for workers and their families".

He added: "While it is worrying, going into administration does give other potential buyers the opportunity to engage and we will work hard with all the relevant parties to ensure the best possible outcome."

Defence Secretary Wes Streeting said protecting staff is the government's top priority.

"I'm also very mindful that for lots of people across the country, Poundland is a place they go on the high street to get bargains and... given the cost of living challenges we face, that is of concern to us in government," he told the BBC.

Alix Partners, which has been appointed as administrator, said it would look at all options for the business, "including sales of the business and its assets, either in whole or part".

"We appreciate this is a deeply concerning time for all involved - customers, suppliers and, in particular, Poundland colleagues," they said.

Gordon Brothers has been criticised over how it handled a potential sale of Poundland, with accusations the investment firm hoped to profit from selling off the chain's 600 shops and stock piecemeal after running it into the ground.

Gordon Brothers, which specialises in buying and restructuring distressed companies, has denied such claims.

On the funding question, Gordon Brothers said it had loaned the firm Â£85m over a year to help save it from insolvency.

But insiders critical of the US firm have said it stands to make its loans back and more from the insolvency process.

Previously, Gordon Brothers netted millions from liquidating the stock of fashion retailer LK Bennett, and in insolvency fees.

Labour MP Liam Byrne told the BBC the House of Commons' Business, Innovation, Science and Trade Committee will recall Gordon Brothers before Parliament - two weeks after Gordon Brothers representatives last appeared at the committee to answer questions about Poundland, and where MPs said they were "deeply concerned" about the business.

Byrne said: "I am really worried that a 'greed is good' approach to business by its owners has forced [Poundland] into an administration."

He added the committee wants to know "how much money they're ripping out and crucially, how do we make sure, as a country, that we don't have this kind of scenario unfold again".

Poundland's co-founder Steve Smith said the administration "should not be happening". He told the BBC the chain has undergone a turnaround since being taken over by Gordon Brothers, returning to profitability.

He said: "Everybody was behind Poundland. It had turned round. And with buyers on the table, why was it put into administration?"

Gordon Brothers met Business Secretary Jonathan Reynolds on Monday to update him on the process.

The firm said on Wednesday that, despite the Poundland management bringing about a turnaround over the past year, the firm was "still vulnerable" to inflation and any downturn in sales or profit margin, and it had faced "several years of instability".

A spokesperson added the firm's "thoughts are with Poundland colleagues".

It said it had stepped in to take over the retailer in 2025 when it was struggling, and when "no-one else was willing to finance the business".

One of the central stumbling blocks in negotiations to keep the business afloat was a shareholder loan Gordon Brothers inherited when it bought the business for less than Â£1 last year.

Gordon Brothers was owed Â£22.5m of that loan, while the previous owner Pepco was owed Â£7.5m.

Gordon Brothers rejected various offers to buy the business as a going concern with deals to pay off that loan.

Irish firm Martin Property Group offered to pay off Â£5m of the loan up front, and defer the rest of the payments to 2030.

London-based Xyra group offered Â£12.5m up front but ran out of time to get a deal over the line.

German investment firm Zachert Private Equity is also understood to have made a late offer.

The crisis at Poundland really came to a head over the past few weeks after Gordon Brothers restricted access to funds for day-to-day operations.

This meant that stock at the retailer was not being replenished at the necessary pace in the vital run-up to the festive period.

#132. Secondhand shopping is going mainstream this holiday season as consumers seek value

As the high cost of living squeezes consumers' wallets this holiday season, more shoppers are turning to secondhand retail and thrifting.

The shift comes as consumers look for better value, buyers put more emphasis on personalization and younger generations change the way they shop. The U.S. secondhand apparel market grew 13% in 2025, nearly four times faster than the overall retail clothing market, according to GlobalData.

That segment is expected to reach nearly $80 billion by 2030, growing more than 7% annually on average, GlobalData found. Much of that expansion — 40% — was driven by Generation Z, followed closely by millennials.

"I think this has been their version of the thrill of the hunt that millennials loved during the recession days," Katherine Cullen, a global senior retail analyst at EY, told CNBC. "It's a way to really express an individual style, find something unique, something that really fits their own vibe and personality."

It goes beyond people shopping for themselves, Cullen said. With more emphasis on unique holiday gifts, shoppers are leaning more on secondhand retail than they have before, whether in clothing or in other retail categories.

They're looking for something that fits the personality of the person they're shopping for, she added.

For Vanessa Roberts, that meant finding two antique children's chairs that she says will perfectly round out her nieces' play space. Roberts, 37, said she's been thrifting for at least two decades, each time embracing the "thrill" of finding something unique or vintage.

Especially in fashion, at a time when the quality of mass-produced items has decreased, thrifted items offer more value beyond just the price tag, Roberts added.

"It's crazy that you can find better quality items at the thrift store right now from 20 years ago versus what you can find at stores right now," she said.

Roberts, who lives in Los Angeles, said she began shopping for holiday gifts in September. As she prepares her haul for December, roughly 70% of her gifting will come from secondhand stores, she estimated.

"It's been a fun little challenge, where it's like a little scavenger hunt when I go into the thrift store," she said. "I'm definitely thrifting for the holidays – I do it every year."

The trend coincides with an economy that has forced consumers to be more intentional about how they spend, as they face high inflation and elevated gas prices.

In October, the consumer price index rose a seasonally adjusted 0.4%, which was more than anticipated. Still, consumers are expected to continue spending through the holiday season, with sales projected to top $1 trillion for the first time — though some of that estimated increase is boosted by inflation.

"You have a consumer who, while they're willing to spend at the top level, they're willing to splurge selectively," Cullen said. "Consumers are looking for value, which I think is something that is making secondhand resale and re-commerce more attractive."

And while clothing is largely the entry point to buying resale, secondhand shopping is increasingly expanding into furniture, electronics and books, according to a report from consumer insights firm The Consumer Collective.

Its October report found that 34% of people surveyed said secondhand products offer better value than brand new ones.

Many secondhand items also have an inherent added value beyond the low price on the tag, according to eBay CEO Jamie Iannone. The uniqueness of those products, as well as the allure that vintage adds to them, means consumers may actually be seeking the benefits of secondhand in more ways than one.

"I can buy a product that's used, and it almost has cachet around it," Iannone told CNBC. "Consumers today, they're able to get these amazing values on products, and it's seen as very normal to be gifting pre-loved."

According to eBay's Recommerce Report in October, 70% of global consumers surveyed said they consider secondhand shopping important for managing their budgets. That same report also found that 89% of respondents plan to spend the same amount or more on secondhand products next year compared with this year.

Iannone said he thinks the interest in secondhand this holiday season is "much stronger" than last year.

"It's becoming just a more established part of consumers' shopping and gifting routines," he said.

At a time when algorithms are driving shopping trends, consumers are also looking for ways to distinguish themselves and their purchases.

That's stretching beyond buying for themselves to purchasing holiday gifts for others, too.

"Secondhand is where they're finding unique items to wear," Alon Rotem, chief strategy officer at ThredUp, told CNBC. The company's holiday survey found that respondents valued uniqueness nearly as much as the value of a thrifted piece.

"To me, that represents a pretty meaningful shift in consumer taste," he added.

It's partly why ThredUp, which is an online thrift marketplace, emphasizes that each of its products is "a snowflake," Rotem said — something unique.

That's the appeal of buying preowned, rather than purchasing off the rack that hundreds of other people are reaching for, too.

"Consumer tastes have changed, and they're much more into vintage," he added. "Uniqueness has a premium in their minds."

Larger retailers are taking note, EY's Cullen said. More retailers, such as Lululemon, Patagonia and Madewell, are dabbling in preowned.

Wall Street is also increasingly watching the growing secondhand trend. Though shares of some secondhand and resale companies such as eBay and Savers Value Village have climbed this year, shares of both ThredUp and The RealReal have tumbled year to date.

"There's a lot of conversation around authenticity or the best find," Cullen said. "I think that interest and momentum, along with some of the economic factors that we're all facing, the idea that people are looking to be frugal is going to just continue."

#131. Car finance compensation payments explained

Millions of drivers who were mis-sold car finance agreements could face further delays to compensation after the financial regulator said it had made some errors in its calculations.

Driver have already been told they must wait until at least 2027 to receive compensation, although some may be offered an early settlement.

Average payments of about Â£829 are expected under the rules published by the Financial Conduct Authority (FCA).

However, legal challenges to the scheme - which will be heard in February 2027 -mean compensation calculations and payments have been delayed.

The vast majority of new cars, and many second-hand ones, are bought with finance agreements. Customers pay an initial deposit to secure the vehicle, then a monthly fee with interest.

Compensation could be given to many of those who took out a car loan between April 2007 and November 2024.

The decision by the FCA, the financial regulator, applies to about 12 million car loans - about 37% of the total number during the period.

In 2021, the FCA banned deals where car dealers received commission from lenders, based on the interest rate charged to the customer. These were known as discretionary commission arrangements (DCAs) and customers were often not told about them.

The FCA said this provided an incentive for a buyer to be charged a higher-than-necessary interest rate, leaving them paying too much.

Other car buyers were also judged to have signed unfair contracts because the commission paid to the dealer was so high - accounting for 39% of the total cost of credit and 10% of the loan.

Some customers were not given accurate information about the best finance deal because of exclusive arrangements between car dealers and lenders.

Under the latest proposals, the FCA expects average payouts of Â£829 per mis-sold agreement.

The total cost of the compensation, including administrative costs, could hit Â£9.1bn.

How much individual consumers receive will depend on the degree of harm suffered.

The FCA has announced some claimants might be offered an early settlement, but only if it as good as what would be paid under the current proposed central scheme.

Complaints have already been made about four million finance agreements. Those people do not need to do anything.

The regulator urged anyone who has not yet complained to contact their car loan provider directly, rather than using a third-party claims management company.

The regulator's central compensation scheme allows people to complain and potentially receive compensation for mis-sold deals without the need for a lawyer or to go through the courts.

Motorists have also been warned to be on the alert for scammers posing as car finance lenders offering fake compensation.

The FCA has published this guidance on how to complain, external.

lenders will respond to claims, explaining if you are owed compensation and how much â but timing of those letters is now uncertain owing to the legal challenge

those who complain before the scheme gets up and running are likely to receive compensation faster

some people who complained by 30 June and are not owed compensation should be told by 18 November, if the agreement began on or after 1 April 2014 and you complained by 30 June 2026

some of those who complained by 31 August will be told if they are not owed compensation by 18 January 2027, if the agreement began before 1 April 2014

there are exemptions, external, and anyone who does not hear from their lender by the dates set out, isn't necessarily owed compensation

those who have not complained will be contacted by their lender. People will be asked if they want to opt in to the scheme to have their case reviewed

those motor finance borrowers who do not receive a letter - for example because lenders no longer have their details and cannot trace them - can still make a claim

Regulators have warned claims management companies and law firms involved in motor finance commission claims to make sure consumers do not have multiple representatives for the same claim and are not charged excessive termination fees.

FCA boss Nikhil Rathi told the BBC's Today programme there are "many law firms out there who would like to get 30% of any compensation", stressing that the regulator's scheme was "free to use" for consumers.

Millions of drivers were in line to receive compensation this year, and most of the remainder should have got compensation by the end of 2027.

But the FCA has confirmed that no compensation will be paid before 2027 as a result of legal challenges to the scheme.

The watchdog has now said it uncovered some mistakes in how it categorised a small group of loan agreements under its redress scheme, which could have a small impact on compensation levels.

"While the potential impact on average compensation is small â less than Â£10 per agreement, on average â we recognise this affects the ongoing litigation," the FCA said. That could leave average payouts somewhere between Â£819 and Â£839.

"We will discuss with the challengers and the court the impact on this deadline, and for the timeline of the litigation."

Consumer Voice has said the scheme leaves "too many people short-changed". The FCA has also received challenges from three lenders: Volkswagen Financial Services, Mercedes Benz Financial Services, and Credit Agricole Auto Finance.

The UK's Upper Tribunal has agreed to hear legal challenges to the scheme, with a hearing scheduled for 16 February to 3 March.

It means that lenders will no longer need to calculate or pay compensation to people owed money under its scheme, until the legal process concludes.

The FCA said it would need to decide what to do next if the courts decided to overturn the programme. Without a scheme in place, the FCA has estimated that up to 19 million complaints would need to be handled individually, taking three years and costing lenders Â£6bn more.

It said it would "defend the scheme robustly as lawful and the best way to resolve such a widespread, long running and complex issue".

Ultimately, the industry is expected to cover the full costs of any compensation scheme, including any administrative costs.

Lenders - including some of the UK's biggest banks and specialist motor finance firms - have already set aside billions of pounds for potential payouts.

The FCA said a minority of claimants will be made early offers, but most will have to wait for the outcome of next year's court case.

With huge compensation payouts inevitable, Barclays will offer early settlements to some claimants, with others expected to follow suit.

However, the FCA said it had asked Barclays to pause making compensation offers while it worked the errors it identified in November.

The FCA has published a factsheet to help drivers, external work out whether they should accept early settlements from lenders.

Accepting would mean quicker payouts but remove them from any changes to the central scheme.

The body that represents the lending industry, the Finance and Leasing Association, said it had "concerns" about the central programme but that it was choosing not to raise a legal challenge.

Santander, Barclays and Lloyds also accepted the scheme, despite raising concerns that the level of redress is disproportionate to those who suffered harm.

Even if drivers are entitled to compensation from these lenders they will need to wait.

There were some concessions made to lenders in a scaled-down final compensation plan from the FCA.

The Supreme Court considered three test cases which influenced the FCA's decision and, ultimately, limited how broad the compensation programme could have been.

It focused on whether the car dealers had a duty to act on behalf of their customers, rather than in their own interests. The test case which was upheld was that of Marcus Johnson, who bought his first car - a Suzuki Swift - in 2017.

In his case, the Supreme Court said the terms of his finance deal were unfair due of the size of the commission payment, and the fact he appeared to have been misled over the relationship between the finance firm and the dealer.

Have you been turned down for compensation relating to a car finance deal? Share your experiences.
