#130. New UK-EU treaty has 10-year termination clause

The government and the EU have published the texts of two treaties agreed last week as part of a post-Brexit reset.

The treaties were published on Thursday afternoon, alongside legislation designed to enact the bulk of the deal.

The deal covering food exports has a 10-year termination clause that would mean if one party ended the agreement, they would have to pay "reasonable and proportionate costs" to rebuild border checks infrastructure, which will be significantly scaled back as part of the new deal.

Opposition leader Kemi Badenoch has said she would scrap the deal while Reform UK's Nigel Farage has suggested he would refuse to pay.

The Brexit reset deal has already sparked debate about its benefits, its costs, and a wider discussion on the future of the UK-EU relationship.

The deal covers British food exports, emissions trading and a youth experience scheme.

Two of the three legal texts were released on Thursday, covering the food trade and emissions trading scheme deals, alongside the government's cost-cutting bill to enact the deal.

A new assessment of the impact of the deal on different sectors and regions of the country was also released.

EU offers UK fresh deal on 'Made in Europe' scheme

Badenoch challenges Burnham over university fees for EU students

Farage helps launch new campaign against the UK rejoining the EU

Under the deal, food exporters will be significant beneficiaries from the scrapping of considerable red tape and checks, and dismantling of border facilities. An internal analysis forecasts a 16% boost the export of major agricultural commodities.

The industry and government hope the reduction in costs for food producers and sellers will be passed on to customers, but there may be no clear visible impact at the checkout.

Privately, industry insiders told the BBC earlier this week that there are too many moving parts in the world economy, in particular the energy price shock resulting from war in the Middle East.

Regarding the fine detail in the text of one of the treaties relating to the 10-year termination clause, European affairs minister Hamish Falconer said: "The Cutting Costs Bill allows us to implement two major trade agreements."

"It allows us to eliminate paperwork for our food and drink traders, protect billions of pounds worth of British exports from carbon taxes, and make doing business with our largest trading partner cheaper and easier."

A plan to link British and European emissions trading schemes will see more coverage across the UK's aviation and maritime industries.

The treaty text also contains finer detail of how the UK will observe, shape but not vote on relevant EU rules. The UK will be invited to attend European Commission meetings and expert groups as an observer, as well as taking part in emergency food security planning.

However, the UK will not be given voting rights.

Plans to use secondary legislation, including granting direct so-called "Henry VIII powers" to ministers to align UK and EU laws, are detailed in the legislation.

Such powers will enable ministers to make changes to bills with less Parliamentary scrutiny.

Outlining the agreement with the EU to MPs earlier this week, the prime minister said new agreements with the EU are "pragmatic" and "not a prelude to anything".

Andy Burnham also said it would be "unthinkable" for the UK to rejoin the bloc without a referendum.

Several former prime ministers have urged him not to reopen the Brexit debate. Burnham said they are "stuck in the past" and later claimed there would be "no banging on about Europe" from Labour.

Badenoch said Burnham has "oversold" the deal and was "trying to take Britain back to 2015".

#128. Drivers over 70 face eye tests every three years under new plans

Over-70s will have to take eye tests every three years or face losing their driving licence under new road safety plans.

Mandatory eyesight tests are part of the Department for Transport's (DfT) road safety strategy, expected within weeks.

Around 6.5 million over-70s hold drivers' licences, with 1.6 million of those aged 80 or over.

To drive legally in the UK, you must be able to read a standard number plate from 20 metres away, wearing glasses or contact lenses if required.

Government sources told the BBC ministers will recommend over-70s be made to regularly prove their eyesight meets the minimum standards. Those who do not, or who fail to meet the minimum standard face losing their licence.

The change follows a consultation which launched early last year, set to recommend a shake-up of the rules for older drivers.

Under the current rules, over 70s have to renew their licence every three years, but only have to self-certify that their sight.

Dennis Reed, of campaign group Silver Voices, which represents older people, said it was "discriminatory to just pick an age out of the hat and say 'everybody over 70 has to go through this, but nobody else does'".

But he said the group is not opposed in principle to regular eye tests for older drivers.

The changes being considered were first reported by the Daily Mail, external.

Drivers over 70 to face eye tests every three years

The NHS provides free eye tests for over 60s, but they are not detailed enough to approve someone to drive.

The BBC understands this system is also being looked at, with the free tests likely to be expanded to become more rigorous.

A Government source said "beefed up measures" were long overdue.

"One life lost on the roads is one too many," they added. "There have been several arguably preventable fatalities on Britain's roads caused by drivers with failing eyesight in recent years.

The DfT said no decision has officially been made on mandating eyesight tests for drivers aged 70 and over.

A spokesperson said: "We recognise the importance of driving for older people's independence but need to balance this with our priority to keep everyone safe on our roads and avoid tragic accidents.

"Mandatory eye tests for drivers over 70 as part of our Road Safety Strategy are one way to do this.

"We are carefully reviewing consultation results," they added. "Older drivers who are fit to drive and have good enough vision have nothing to worry about."

#126. Norse Atlantic suspends all flights as it faces potential collapse

The Norwegian low cost carrier Norse Atlantic Airways has suspended all flights as it rushes to resolve what it warned is a critical financial situation.

The airline said if no solution can be found, it intends to file for bankruptcy.

The airline uses a fleet of 12 Boeing 787 aircraft on long-haul routes from a number of European airports, including London Gatwick, Rome and Oslo.

Prior to the suspension of flights, it was advertising services from London to Orlando, Cape Town, Bangkok and Phuket.

One aircraft was meant to depart London for Orlando this morning, while another was meant to arrive from Cape Town this evening.

The suspension has also affected Pakistan International Airlines, which had been leasing aircraft and crews from Norse Atlantic.

PIA was forced to cancel services from London to Islamabad and from Manchester to Lahore last night.

In a statement on social media, PIA said all UK flights from today would be operated by aircraft from its own fleet.

Londoner Fred was due to fly to Cape Town with Norse Atlantic next week for a friend's wedding, but has decided to change his plans.

"Yesterday the exact same flight I was going to get was cancelled, so I was worried" he told the BBC's Your Voice. "I sent an email to Norse and have had nothing back".

He decided to rebook with a different airline, which cost him an extra Â£1,000.

Another traveller, Claire from Herefordshire, is currently on holiday in South Africa with her daughter.

"We are due to fly home on Sunday and are now looking at a really expensive flight back," she said.

"We have tried contacting Norse but have been given an automated response saying someone will get back to us within three business days."

Consumer group Which? advised Norse Atlantic customers to not cancel their flights as the airline has not collapsed. Doing so removes passengers' right to a refund.

Rory Boland, Which? travel editor, said: "If Norse does go bust, whether you get a refund depends on how you booked and paid. If you booked directly with Norse, you may be able to claim a refund via your credit card provider, or possibly your debit card."

Those stuck abroad should contact insurers, while those with package holidays reliant on Norse Atlantic flights should contact their tour operator, he added.

Norse Atlantic Airways was set up in 2021 as a low-cost long-haul carrier.

It entered the market as a successor to Norwegian, which withdrew from long haul routes as a result of the Covid pandemic.

But it has struggled to make money, and its problems worsened this year, as fuel prices rocketed due to the war in Iran.

Unlike many other European carriers, Norse Atlantic does not hedge its fuel bills by buying supplies in advance at fixed prices.

This left it fully exposed when prices soared, with the cost of fuel roughly doubling at one point. Prices remain significantly higher than they were before the conflict began.

In the first half of the year Norse Atlantic made a loss of $94.6m.

In a statement to the Oslo Stock Exchange on Wednesday, the airline said its financial situation was critical, and it needed to find short term funding to continue its operations.

After suspending flights, the company said it was in discussions with investors regarding a possible takeover that "would include a financing solution".

However, it said if short term funding could not be found quickly, "it is the Board's intention to file for bankruptcy".

Norse Atlantic had been looking for a buyer since July.

Its potential collapse follows the failure of Spirit Airlines in May. The US-based low cost carrier was another airline left exposed when jet fuel prices soared, as US airlines typically do not hedge their purchases.

If Norse Atlantic were to fold, it would be another major setback for the low-cost long-haul model, which has typically proved popular with passengers but difficult to sustain financially, especially on transatlantic routes.

#125. Macy's postpones Thanksgiving Day Parade due to severe weather

Macy's on Wednesday announced it's postponing its Thanksgiving Day Parade, originally scheduled for Thursday, due to severe weather.

The parade, held each year on Thanksgiving morning, will now take place on Saturday at 8:30 a.m. ET, according to the company. The New York City area is expected to have heavy rain and thunderstorms on Thanksgiving.

"The safety of our participants and spectators is our top priority, and after close consultation with our city and agency partners, we have made the decision to move this year's celebration," Macy's said in a statement.

This year's event marks the 100th Macy's Thanksgiving Day Parade. The annual event has never been canceled due to weather, though it was suspended between 1942 and 1944 for World War II.

The parade's viability this year had been in question after concerns arose over strong winds that could become dangerous for the massive balloons that the event is known for. Many of the balloons had already been inflated as of Wednesday afternoon.

The postponement also means thousands of volunteers and artists slated to perform will have to adapt to Saturday's schedule.

#123. Guitar wars - the transatlantic battle over the Stratocaster

The Fender Stratocaster is widely considered to be the best-known shape in electric guitars

Hans Thomann leads the way through his music store in central Germany to the large electric guitar department, where the walls are hung with hundreds of instruments.

Brands on show include famous names Fender and Gibson, plus Harley Benton.

The latter is a low-price range of guitars owned by the family-run company that Thomann leads â Thomann, one of the world's largest online retailers of musical instruments.

The shop, together with warehouses and offices, are at the company's headquarters in the tiny village of Treppendorf in rural Upper Franconia, in Bavaria.

The business was founded by Thomann's father, Hans Thomann Senior, in 1954.

In one corner of the store, a young man is trying out a Fender Stratocaster, one of the world's most iconic electric guitars.

Designed in the US in the early 1950's, the "Strat" became the instrument of choice for many famous rock and pop musicians, such as Jimi Hendrix, Eric Clapton and Buddy Holly.

Hendrix famously set one on fire at the Monterey Pop Festival in 1967, and his performances helped make the guitar a symbol of modern rock music.

Hans Thomann says that Fender's letter came as a big surprise

These days, the Stratocaster is very much on Hans Thomann's mind, because it's the subject of an intense copyright dispute.

When the guitar was first made, California-based Fender did not trademark the body shape. So, since the 1960s Strat-shaped guitars have been made and sold around the world by countless manufacturers.

The shape is so ubiquitous that many musicians consider it to be a generic design.

But since March of this year, Fender has begun a new campaign to defend its right to the guitar's sinuous shape. It follows a decision by a court in DÃ¼sseldorf last December which granted Fender copyright protection, external of the Stratocaster design in Germany.

The defendant, Chinese seller Yiwu Philharmonic Musical Instruments, did not appear. The DÃ¼sseldorf court accepted Fender's arguments and ruled in its favour. According to German law the court makes its decision based on the facts presented by the plaintiff if the defendant doesn't raise objections.

The ruling said that Fender's design for the Stratocaster was "an outstanding, free creative achievement" that "clearly reflects the personality of its author".

It described the guitar as "a body without edges" with "soft curves that evoke associations with the female torso consisting of hips, waist and arms".

On its website, Fender announced in March, external the decision created "enforceable rights" against any guitars using the Strat body shape, that are manufactured, sold or distributed into Germany or other EU countries, "regardless of where those guitars are produced".

Fender began to send out cease and desist letters to dozens of retailers and manufacturers, both large and small, asking them to stop making and selling Strat lookalikes heading for Europe.

One of these letters, seen by the BBC, said Fender "insists that you immediately stop manufacturing, marketing, selling and producing the infringing products".

The letter also demanded that companies recall and destroy the guitars.

Jimi Hendrix helped to make the Fender Stratocaster famous

The move caused an outcry in the guitar world, among manufacturers and musicians.

"Time to boycott Fender," one person wrote on the Facebook page of the boutique US guitar maker, LSL Instruments.

Thomann received a cease-and-desist letter shortly after a visit to Treppendorf in March by Fender's chief executive Bud Cole.

"We remember the meeting as a very positive and constructive exchange," says Thomann. "At the time we had no indication that Fender was considering legal action."

"For more than 70 years, we have carried Fender instruments with great appreciation for the brand and many of us are Fender players ourselves. We were therefore surprised and disappointed by Fender's recent actions."

In 2025, Thomann sold over 112,000 Strat-style guitars, including more than 16,000 made by Fender and Fender subsidiary brand Squier.

"As a retailer, we want to continue offering our customers everything that is available in the world of guitars," adds Thomann. "We would have welcomed to the opportunity to discuss the matter openly before receiving a formal legal demand for such extreme action."

Thomann has just one physical shop, but sells online around the world

In June, Thomann the company started legal action against Fender, filing an "action of non-infringement" that said that Fender does not have a valid copyright claim to the Stat shape.

Thomann says that the DÃ¼sseldorf ruling was simply a default judgement based on missed deadlines and didn't represent a "comprehensive review of the legal claims".

For its part, Fender, which is majority-owned by Hawaii-based company Servco Pacific, says it has a responsibility to protect the Stratocaster's iconic design. In July, it sued Thomann in a German court, for copyright infringement.

In a statement to the BBC, Fender said it remained "confident" in the original DÃ¼sseldorf court judgement.

"Our preference has always been to keep lines of communication open and resolve these matters through constructive dialogue rather than litigation," it added.

With the legal cases continuing, Thomann says he believes it is important to get "legal clarity â not only for us but for the wider guitar industry."

"Ultimately, we want to help preserve an environment in which innovation, diversity and creative development can continue to thrive," he adds.

The Stratocaster is the most used emoji for a guitar

Thomann says he was taking legal action not just for his company, but for smaller music companies too.

"We used to be a small music shop ourselves," he says.

"Many of the smaller manufacturers, guitar makers and dealers affected simply don't have the financial and legal resources to conduct a legal dispute like this."

One such company is LSL Instruments, a family-owned boutique guitar maker in California. Its director of sales, Johny Miller, told me that when they first received Fender's letter they were "pretty terrified and blown away".

"They were asking us to retrieve guitars from dealers out there and destroy them and send them proof.

"So when we heard Hans Thomann got the same letter and responded by suing themâ¦ so that everybody in the guitar community could understand exactly what's going onâ¦ we were so thankful and grateful," he says.

Fender tells BBC that it agrees that "legal clarity was important", and that it was now focusing more on targeting larger manufacturers.

It also says it realises that "our outreach has caused concern and frustration, particularly among smaller, independent guitar builders. "To them we say: we continue to hear you, and we're listening."

#121. SpaceX, Musk pledge to donate a share each to active Trump Accounts

SpaceX said Wednesday it will donate one share of the company's class A stock to every active Trump Account, and CEO Elon Musk would personally match the gift.

"SpaceX is building the infrastructure of the future and working to make life multiplanetary," the company said in a statement. "We believe ownership of the company creating these systems should extend to the next generation of Americans for their long-term benefit."

The White House announced in October that nearly 70 million Trump Accounts had been created, including more than 60 million through auto-enrollment. It's unclear how many are considered active.

Musk said in a social media post on X on Thursday that the share from SpaceX would be transferred next month, without providing further details. "The share directly from me will be transferred when my lockup expires in June next year," he added.

Eligible children must have their account claimed to enable contributions from family members, friends and employers, and to receive the government's one-time $1,000 seed contribution. Auto-enrolled children can receive philanthropic contributions without a parent or guardian claiming an account, according to TrumpAccounts.gov.

Representatives for SpaceX and the U.S. Department of the Treasury did not immediately respond to a request for further details about the planned gift.

SpaceX Chief Operating Officer Gwynne Shotwell had previously agreed to make a personal gift of over 2 million SpaceX shares to Trump Accounts, with eligible account holders expected to receive them in December.

In October, Elon Musk posted on X that he would give a share of SpaceX to every kid in America next year.

Trump Accounts, also known as 530A accounts, are open to any U.S. child under 18 with a Social Security number.

Parents, guardians, grandparents and others can collectively contribute up to $5,000 per child per year in after-tax dollars up until the year before the beneficiary turns 18. The annual contribution limit is indexed for inflation after 2027.

Since the July 4 launch, families and friends have collectively contributed more than $1 billion to Trump Accounts. Total deposits now sit at more than $9 billion, CNBC first reported.

Children born from 2025 through 2028 may receive a one-time $1,000 deposit from the Treasury. Other funds may also be available, depending on certain criteria.

The Treasury rolled out temporary regulations at the end of September that allow Trump Accounts to hold stock donations, a move officials have said is meant to encourage more philanthropic gifts.

Contributing stock can benefit donors, who are able to avoid capital gains taxes triggered by selling appreciated stock. But families can face restrictions related to selling that donated stock. The policy also means some children's wealth accumulation could be tied to the performance of individual companies.

Under U.S. Department of the Treasury guidelines, families cannot refuse stock gifted to Trump Accounts and generally cannot sell it until the earlier of five years or the end of the calendar year in which the beneficiary turns 17.

SpaceX shares have been under some pricing pressure as the company negotiates a possible merger with Elon Musk's automaker Tesla. The companies have established two special committees to handle merger negotiations, and hired advisers for a potential mega-deal.

CNBC's Sam Meredith, Kevin Breuninger, Kate Dore and Jess Dickler contributed to this story

#119. When will Poundland shops close? Five key questions answered

Poundland has collapsed into administration, leaving the jobs of its 11,000 staff in doubt in the run-up to Christmas.

The company has appointed consultancy Alix Partners to oversee a sale of part or all of the discount retailer, with questions remaining about the future of the company.

This is the question that is most difficult to answer.

Poundland shops will remain open as usual for now. The firm collapsing has acted as a reset in the process of selling it.

Administrators are looking to either sell the business as a whole, or to break it up, which is the more likely outcome.

When Poundland was still a going concern, Irish property firm Martin Property Group made an offer for it. But a source said the group was now "unlikely" to make a fresh bid due to "irreparable harm" to the brand from the administration process.

Before the collapse, Fortress, which owns rival budget chain Poundstretcher, was interested in buying about 200 of the stores, and wanted to keep those trading under the Poundland brand.

But Fortress's position at the moment is unclear. The BBC understands it is considering selling Poundstretcher, a move first reported by Bloomberg.

A decision on Poundstretcher could come before the end of this week. However, Fortress has declined to comment.

When the chain was sold by Pepco last year for Â£1 it was struggling. It suffered a Â£68m loss that year.

Poundland's managing director Barry Williams said Poundland had been seeing a "strong recovery" and recorded a profit by one measure in the first nine months of this year.

Poundland's management paint a picture of a retailer that was turning a corner until the owner, US investment firm Gordon Brothers, pulled the plug on funding for day-to-day expenses such as buying stock.

The Usdaw union, which represents shopworkers, claim the owners forced Poundland into administration.

Labour MP Antonia Bance said she was "pretty appalled" by the conduct of Gordon Brothers over the past few weeks, and that it was going to make much bigger profits by breaking up the business rather than by keeping it going.

However, Gordon Brothers insisted the business was only turning a profit on paper, and had missed budget forecasts.

A spokeswoman said while the management team had made Â£100m in cost reductions, the business had moved from a Â£70m loss to "around break-even".

"Break-even is different from generating the cash needed to buy stock, invest, and withstand increasing economic pressures," the spokeswoman said. "A few good weeks do not make a turnaround."

Poundland repeatedly missed budget targets over the year, the spokeswoman added.

Gordon Brothers was concerned that as inflation goes up next year, those pressures would have helped to make the business unviable, it said.

And Julie Palmer, managing partner at insolvency agency Begbies Traynor, said Poundland's collapse "seemed somehow inevitable" due to challenges facing retailers - including higher costs to employ staff.

Stores are still trading while the administrators work out if the business is viable.

The funds locked up by Gordon Brothers - at the last count about Â£70m - have now been released by the administrators.

This has turned the taps back on, so staff will continue to be paid in the run-up to Christmas, and so will suppliers.

But here's the catch: the administrators have a legal obligation to maximise value for "secured" creditors. It means the owners, Gordon Brothers, are at the head of the queue for being paid back the loans that were stumbling blocks in the negotiations.

But more importantly for staff, if the administrators can get more value by breaking up the business than by selling it as a whole, then that is what they have to do, even if the business would be viable.

If the business is broken up and its stores are sold off, the new owners would not be obliged to keep staff on.

From 26 November you will not get a refund for goods if you change your mind about the purchase. If the item is faulty you will still get a refund.

The immediate reason Poundland collapsed was that Gordon Brothers locked up funds for the day-to-day running of the business, including to pay suppliers.

This meant that stock was running low at Poundland.

But those funds have now been unlocked, meaning suppliers can be paid, and supply chains can be re-established, at least in the short term.

If an item is on the shelves, then shoppers can buy it.

Administrators will have to get the best value for creditors, which could mean selling the business as it stands.

But if Poundland is broken up, administrators could sell the brand without the stores, or the brand along with some of the stores, or the stores without the Poundland brand, to other retailers.

As a result there could be fewer discount stores on the High Street, providing less incentive for other retailers to keep prices down.

"Poundland is a place they go on the High Street to get bargains and... given the cost of living challenges we face, that is of concern to us in government," Wes Streeting, the defence secretary, told the BBC on Thursday.

Poundland's customers may well turn to rival budget retailers in the hunt for bargains.

#117. New boss appointed to lead Travelodge

Peter Krueger will lead Travelodge after a turbulent few months

Travelodge has appointed a former Tui executive to become its new boss from the start of December.

The former CEO of the Oxfordshire-based budget hotel chain Jo Boydell quit in the summer after safety issues were raised at many of its sites.

A woman staying at its Maidenhead hotel was sexually assaulted in December 2022 after a man lied to staff to get a key card to her room. Kyran Smith was jailed for seven-and-a-half years for the attack.

Peter Krueger will start his new role at Travelodge on Tuesday, replacing interim chief Ray Reidy who has led the company since Boydell quit.

She left the company after facing significant criticism over the handling of the sexual assault incident and wider safety concerns.

It prompted former prime minister Sir Keir Starmer to urge her to "seriously engage" with the government on the issue after she cancelled a meeting with MPs.

An independent review by Paul Greaney KC published in October found the sexual assault victim was treated in a "disgraceful" way and "fundamentally failed" by the hotel chain.

In an update on Thursday, Reidy said the business is "continuing to make further investments into room security, and the safety and security of our guests remains our top priority".

Travelodge said it will be making additional investments in room security "over the course of 2027".

#116. Spain’s Pablo Hernández de Cos set to lead European Central Bank

Pablo Hernández de Cos is set to succeed Christine Lagarde as president of the European Central Bank next year.

He received backing from euro zone leaders at the European Council Summit, multiple media reported on Thursday.

The role still requires formal appointment after consultation with the European Parliament and the ECB's Governing Council, but neither has veto power and the decision follows intense political wrangling to reach a consensus on who should serve the eight-year term.

Reuters reported that de Cos was set for the nomination after the Netherlands agreed to back Klaas Knot, the only other candidate, citing European diplomatic sources.

De Cos is a 55-year-old economist and career central banker who rose through the ranks of the Bank of Spain, starting as a researcher in the 1990s and working in various departments before becoming governor from 2018 to June 2024.

He chaired the Basel Committee on Banking Supervision from 2019 to 2024, and has been General Manager of the Basel-based Bank for International Settlements since July 2025.

Both de Cos and his main rival for the ECB top job, Knot, were widely seen as candidates with deep knowledge and experience in monetary policy and financial stability — attributes that took on particular importance at a time of resurgent inflation, fragmentation and political uncertainty.

Lagarde had extensive experience in financial institutions but was criticized by some early in her tenure for a lack of central banking experience.

De Cos gained sufficient political backing in closed-door discussions and negotiations since Lagarde said she would step down on Feb. 7, 2027, over seven years after she became the first woman to helm the ECB. That was despite a final push by the Netherlands at the European Council meeting this week backing Knot.

Representatives from the euro area's biggest economies — Germany, France, Italy, Spain and the Netherlands — have been horse-trading as they look to secure representation on the central bank's six-member Executive Board.

Influential roles as chief economist, expected to go to Germany's Markus Brunnermeier, and head of the Markets Portfolio, for which France's Laurence Boone has reportedly been tapped, also change hands in 2027.

Analysts say de Cos's commentary and actions over more than two decades at Spain's central bank paint him as moderately dovish — inclined to favor lower interest rates to support economic growth — but also as a pragmatist who has been willing to adapt.

That has included support for strong rate hikes during the energy price shock that followed Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine in February 2022, but also giving staunch backing for rates below zero and the huge stimulus package launched by then-ECB President Mario Draghi to reboot the euro zone economy in 2019.

De Cos quipped in 2024 that he was an "owl" after being asked if he was a hawk or a dove, emphasizing his focus on data and the situation at hand.

"It is true that Hernández de Cos supported the ECB's uber-dovish monetary policy during the period when inflation was persistently below target. However, his monetary policy views may well be regime-dependent," Christian Kopf, head of fixed income and foreign exchange at Union Investment, said in emailed comments.

"We are now clearly in a higher-inflation regime, with core inflation in the euro area expected to remain above the ECB's target through 2029."

Kopf added that de Cos was the candidate with the "strongest technical understanding of monetary policy and central banking," which he said was important at a time when the world is "witnessing an assault on central bank independence, not only in the United States but also in countries such as Japan."

"By appointing a career technocrat such as Hernández de Cos as ECB President, the European Council would send a strong signal that Europe will not waver in its commitment to price stability and that the euro will remain a hard currency," Kopf said.

Spain's de Cos as president, Germany's Brunnermeier as chief economist and France's Boone as head of market operations represent a "carefully crafted package deal," Kopf also noted, because it balances representation from northern and southern European countries. The result was partially due to the knock-on effects of the German government pushing hard for the chief economist position, he said.

Along with a desire among politicians for influence, a north-south balance is seen as important as the northern countries are seen as leaning more hawkish — more inclined to opt for rate hikes and restrictive policy to tame inflation — while southern are perceived as more dovish.

John Orchard, chairman of the Digital Monetary Institute at think tank OMFIF, said that as a "central banker's central banker," de Cos had "managed to earn respect from typically hawkish northern observers."

Another supportive factor of de Cos's candidacy was the stellar growth rate of many of those southern economies in recent years.

Once the "sick men" of the euro area's 2010s sovereign debt crisis, Portugal, Italy, Spain and Greece have become some of the bloc's strongest growth drivers in recent years.

Factors such as strong tourism, relative political stability, increased foreign investment and strengthening public finances have boosted southern economies. Meanwhile, the euro zone's traditional powerhouse Germany has stuttered amid soaring industrial costs and a struggling autos market.

Orchard said that some believed that Lagarde's presidency had ushered in an "esprit de corps" at the ECB, with policymakers focused on the interests of the whole European economy.

That would, in theory, reduce the importance of nationality among leadership and of traditional notions of hawkish northern nations and dovish southern ones. However, Orchard also noted the recurrent theme of national representation in the recent debate and the perception of Knot as a hawkish choice.

Diverging performance is a core challenge for any leader of the ECB, which must set monetary policy for 21 economies all growing at different speeds and with their own domestic priorities and inflationary pressures.

But it has become particularly pressing this year as the bloc's second-biggest economy, France, is teetering on the edge of its own potential debt crisis.

Moves in French markets and whether the ECB needs to take more extreme measures beyond continuing to urge fiscal discipline will remain in focus for de Cos.

The ECB Executive Board's view of what measures could be taken to prevent market disorder and under what conditions was a key topic under discussion when choosing who should fill the roles, according to insiders.

Despite political efforts to pass a budget that will reduce its budget deficit to around 5% of GDP, investors have been driving up French borrowing costs on concerns over its mounting public debt-to-GDP ratio, high budget deficit and contracting economy.

Risks are being compounded by political uncertainty ahead of France's presidential election in April, which is stymying business activity and raising wider questions about EU unity as far-right and far-left candidates pull ahead.

Rising French yields and the widening spread in France's borrowing costs over Germany's have led to speculation over whether the ECB might need to deploy tools such as liquidity injections or its never-used Transmission Protection Instrument to protect other countries from contagion and support the euro.

This would be politically controversial unless France agreed to stringent fiscal discipline measures akin to those that southern nations signed up to during the Sovereign Debt Crisis — which could be complicated if far-right presidential frontrunner Marine Le Pen, who has a combative relationship with EU institutions, wins.

Ken Egan, head of European sovereigns at ratings agency KBRA, said fragmentation was always a challenge for the ECB, which does not want to see divergent financing conditions and high sovereign spreads across the bloc.

However, he said that in the case of France he believed we were "some way from having a serious conversation about ECB intervention."

"If anything, sovereign spreads have pulled back a bit and tightened, and some measures of market liquidity have also improved in recent weeks," he said by email.

"That doesn't mean the underlying risks have disappeared, but I think it's important to distinguish between periods of market volatility and a more fundamental breakdown in monetary policy transmission."

De Cos also takes up the mantle as Europe faces yet another surge in price pressures.

Euro area inflation rose to 4% in October, its highest level in more than three years. Energy was the biggest contributor, as the Iran war drives up oil and gas prices and tightens the supply of refined fuels.

That leaves the central bank facing a difficult policy balance, preventing higher energy costs from spreading into wages and wider prices while avoiding unnecessary damage to growth.

De Cos will inherit major supranational projects, including plans for a digital euro and Europe's long-delayed capital-markets integration. In a recent speech, Lagarde warned that each year without capital markets integration meant "more of our savings are invested abroad and more of our growing firms look elsewhere for funding."

France is not the only euro area country heading for a pivotal election next year, with votes in Finland, Greece, Italy, Estonia, Slovakia and Slovenia also expected, adding to the politically uncertain climate.

It is also not the only country grappling with higher government borrowing costs, public debt and a struggle to find fiscal headroom, a growing problem among many developed countries.

The ECB's Financial Stability Review meanwhile warned this month of mounting risks from European investors' growing exposure to the U.S. economy, and equity and credit markets' vulnerability to AI.

Outgoing head Lagarde, meanwhile, told the European Banking Congress in Frankfurt this month that her biggest concern was now cyber threats, an area she said policymakers had not paid enough attention.

"The one [worry] that keeps me awake, and many of you awake ​as well ... ​is ⁠AI applied together with cyber threat," she said.

#114. Simon Jack: Is Poundland's US owner to blame for its collapse?

Watch: Why has Poundland collapsed into administration?

The blame game for the demise of Poundland is in full swing.

The number one target is US-based Gordon Brothers who bought the discount chain for Â£1 and have taken nearly Â£20m out of the business already.

Gordon Brothers stand to get paid back a further Â£22.5m, as their share of an existing Â£30m loan is set to be one of the first debts that the administrators will have to pay out of the proceeds of any sale of the business in whole or in part.

The team running Poundland blame the owners for strangling their ability to turn the business around, saying that their access to Poundland's own cash was restricted, leaving them unable to fully restock ahead of the crucial Christmas period.

They point to improving sales over recent months as proof the business had a future.

MPs also laid into the US firm with members of the Business Committee accusing it of "predatory capitalism" and "asset stripping".

Poundland collapses putting 11,000 jobs at risk

No-one appears to dispute the fact that Gordon Brothers' insistence that they get paid most of their Â£22.5m debt was a major stumbling block to a sale.

Potential buyers balked at paying out millions on a loan that Gordon Brothers inherited for nothing.

Martin Property Group offered Â£5m up front with the balance to be deferred, while Xyra Holdings offered Â£12.5m up front but, according to sources close to the deal, were thought unable to actually pay.

Unsurprisingly perhaps, Gordon Brothers dispute a lot of how they have been characterised.

First, they insist that they lent the business Â£85m to try to keep it alive for the 17 months since they bought it. Without them, this collapse would have happened in June last year.

The Â£20m taken out of the business since then has mostly been interest on that loan.

Despite brief periods of sales growth compared with a year earlier, Poundland was not generating enough cash to sustain itself. Sales were forecast to be Â£115m lower than predicted when Gordon Brothers bought the business - more than offsetting a Â£100m cost-cutting programme.

Like other retailers Poundland was hit by rising labour costs, employment taxes and business rates.

In the end, argue Gordon Brothers, none of the potential buyers could meet their terms and prove they had the money and expertise to make a go of it.

Such is the discrepancy between the two versions that the chair of the Business Committee wants Gordon Brothers and the management back in front of them to get to the truth.

It is still possible that a bidder may emerge in the coming hours and days to save the business as a whole but most industry observers think that looks unlikely.

Julie Palmer of insolvency specialists Begbies Traynor told the BBC: "This sort of direction of travel seemed somehow inevitable. Obviously, if one of those interested parties steps up and buys the whole business and assets, that's the best, quickest and cleanest outcome. I think realistically, though, there is a chance now that there will be a breakup."

Like previous busts such as Woolworths, BHS and Wilko, many of these stores will find new owners and they will need local, experienced staff.

But none of that will be much comfort to those workers facing grave uncertainty a month before Christmas, when they should be at their busiest and most needed.

Gordon Brothers specialise in situations of commercial distress. When they take on a distressed company, they try to make sure that the worst possible outcome is still one that makes money for them.

In this situation, the worst outcome would mean they get paid back Â£22.5m, earn interest on the loan they provided the company (secured against the company assets) and a few other miscellaneous fees.

Little wonder then that many people say they had little incentive to strain every sinew to secure a buyer. They argue they kept a business that would have collapsed last June going for another 17 months, which extended employment for thousands of workers.

But if, as some retail veterans have told the BBC, Gordon Brothers never really intended to turn this business around, why would the previous owners PepCo give them a no-lose gamble? Why didn't they do what Gordon Brothers are doing - profiting with no risk?

The answer is simple: Pepco have other businesses, which could have got tangled up in the administration of Poundland.

#112. Millions of people to see average monthly mortgage payments rise by £100

More people face higher mortgage repayments when they roll off a fixed deal than previously estimated, new figures from the the Bank of England show.

About 6.5 million owners are forecast to see their monthly repayment jump by Â£100 on average when they move onto their next deal by the end of 2029, new data in its Financial Stability Report reveals.

That is more the double the Â£45-a-month hike that the Bank estimated in its last report in July.

In the shorter term, about one million of those homeowners, who are on mortgage rates below 3% and due to refinance by the end of next year, will see an average Â£200 increase in their monthly repayments.

For borrowers, the interest rate on a fixed mortgage does not change until it expires, usually after two or five years, and a new one is chosen to replace it. The vast majority of homeowners and buyers have this kind of mortgage.

Since the Iran war began, global economic uncertainty has been pushing up the cost of deals, with the average rates on two and five year fixed mortgages at their highest for about three years.

On Thursday, the average rate on a two-year deal was 5.99%, and at 6% for a five-year deal, according to the financial information service Moneyfacts.

Analysts have described the situation as "brutal" for homeowners coming to the end of their fixed deals.

However, the Bank's report points out that the average Â£100 increase in monthly repayments was half the rise experienced by those refinancing during 2023 and 2024.

Dawn Fazackerley-King is a Pilates instructor who lives near Ipswich and has previously spoken to the BBC about her struggles with the cost of living.

She was on a five-year fixed deal at 1.4%, which she took out in January 2022. But the mother-of-two is about to refix for two years and the best deal she can find is a 5.69% loan. That will push her costs up by about Â£200 a month.

A modern browser with JavaScript and a stable internet connection is required to view this interactive.

At this rate, your payments could change byâ¦

It should be used as a guide only and does not represent the suitability, eligibility or availability of mortgage offers for users. For exact figures, users will need to approach an official mortgage lender.

Interest rates fluctuate based on the Bank of England's base rate and market conditions

She is far from alone, with one million homeowners facing a similar increase often because they are coming off deals that were agreed before interest rates began rising in 2022.

The Bank's report said that households facing higher payments "may need to reduce discretionary spending or draw down savings as higher energy and other essential costs feed through".

Most had savings buffers but lower-income households, including renters, had less wriggle room owing to spending a greater proportion of their income on essentials.

Early in November, the Bank raised the benchmark interest rate, which influences borrowing costs for individuals and businesses, for the first time in more than three years, from 3.75% to 4%.

The numbers could change were mortgage rates to fall, primarily if international economic volatility eased.

Even so, the Bank said around 350,000 were already projected to see monthly mortgage repayments fall when they fixed their next deal within three years.

The report also suggested there were no signs of an increase in borrowers defaulting on their loans, while UK households remained resilient.

"We have not seen the scale of mortgage arrears that we saw if you go back to earlier periods, the early 90s, the early 80s, actually even the financial crisis. And we are not seeing the scale of house repossessions, and that has to be a good thing," said the Bank's governor, Andrew Bailey.

However, separately, the Bank warned that cyber attacks had become a more prominent threat to the UK's financial stability.

Financial firms needed to "keep pace with the growing scale and sophistication of cyber threats", the report said.

#109. Even the price of toys is on the rise. How Americans are scaling back holiday plans

As Amanda Brecht planned how her family would spend Thanksgiving this year, she had to make some difficult decisions.

Because of high gas prices, the 30-year-old decided she wouldn't drive across her home state of Michigan to celebrate with extended family. Instead, Brecht and her husband will spend the holiday with just their three kids. And with food prices rising, they'll be skipping appetizers and serving turkey breast instead of a whole bird.

"Thanksgiving is definitely going to look different this year," Brecht said. "Money doesn't grow on trees. You can only do so much."

With inflation accelerating, Americans like Brecht will see higher prices almost everywhere they look this holiday season. At a time of year synonymous with food, gifts and big spending, many consumers are instead pinching pennies, paring back their plans and making tough choices about how they'll celebrate.

Some are raiding their savings accounts and running up debt on credit cards. They're reporting downbeat outlooks about the economy in surveys. And they've become more selective with spending as inflationary pressures mount.

"Families are facing some tough choices this year," said Alexander Smith, an associate professor who researches behavioral economics at the Worcester Polytechnic Institute. "They might have to cut back on holiday spending in whatever way that they can."

A classic Thanksgiving feast will be harder to pull off as grocery prices rise.

The price of a 10-person Thanksgiving dinner increased 6% to $58.63 in 2026, according to an annual survey from the American Farm Bureau Federation. Faith Parum, the organization's deputy chief economist, said the price increase was a result of higher costs for labor and transportation.

Grocers including Walmart and Aldi have gained traction on social media by offering Thanksgiving meal bundles as a value-focused workaround to higher prices. Walmart's offering promises to feed 10 for $35, and includes a turkey and ingredients to make mashed potatoes, green bean casserole, cranberries, marshmallow-topped sweet potatoes and a pumpkin pie.

Still, some consumers will feel disappointed by having less choice in their menus due to higher prices, according to Joe Bruseulas, chief economist at tax firm RSM.

"We're missing the point here if all we're doing is focusing on the fact that Walmart will sell you a homogeneous dinner meal" at a discount, Bruseulas said. "People feel horrible that they have to go to that because the price of everything has just risen so significantly over the past five years."

Inflation is affecting more than Thanksgiving staples like turkey or stuffing. Food prices on average have jumped around 24% over the last five years, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics. Some items have seen even sharper increases: Coffee and ground beef, for example, have each climbed by more than 40% in the same period despite pulling back from recent highs.

At the same time, millions of Americans lost SNAP benefits under policy changes included in President Donald Trump's "big beautiful bill." Food banks across the U.S. have seen foot traffic skyrocket this year as the reduction in federal nutrition aid coincides with rising prices in grocery aisles.

The Salvation Army said some of its food pantries in October provided double or triple the amount of groceries than they did in the same month a year ago. Demand is now so high that some food banks in Feeding America's national network are worried about running out of supplies.

"We want to make sure that our food banks are as full as they possibly can be going into the holiday season," said Linda Nageotte, Feeding America's president and operations chief. "Far too many of them have empty shelves."

High energy prices as a result of the war with Iran are also affecting Americans' holiday plans.

Slightly fewer Americans will travel at least 50 miles around the Thanksgiving holiday in 2026, marking the first year-over-year decline since the country was in lockdown from the pandemic in 2020, according to AAA. Stacey Barber, a vice president at the auto association, attributed this year's pullback in part to higher fuel costs.

AAA said that about nine out of every 10 Thanksgiving travelers will do so by car. While gas prices have come down from a May peak, they are still up about 18% year over year and could set a new record for the month of November, according to the organization's data.

Travelers are paying about 31% and 23% more for roundtrip domestic airfare around Thanksgiving and Christmas, respectively, than they did a year ago, according to booking platform Hopper. Airlines have blamed the ticket price hikes on rising jet fuel costs in the wake of the war.

"There will be a significant shell shock when they see the price of traveling," said Hayley Berg, lead economist at Hopper.

Berg said price increases would feel more noticeable on routes previously serviced by Spirit, the budget airline that shut down in May. Travelers are booking holiday flights earlier than they did in past years, a sign that prices are top of mind, she said.

Staying home for the holidays isn't a silver-bullet solution, either, as heating costs jump.

The average American household is expected to spend 7% more on home heating this winter than they did last year, according to the National Energy Assistance Directors Association. For the typical family, that equates to an increase of about $66 and a total bill for the season above $1,000.

To be sure, some families will feel it more than others. Heating oil, which is used mainly in homes in the Northeast, has jumped around 70% year over year. However, natural gas, which is one of the most common heating types in the U.S., costs about the same as last year.

Some U.S. states, like Massachusetts and New York, beefed up their home energy support programs to account for higher prices. But Mark Wolfe, NEADA's executive director, said federal aid offerings haven't been meaningfully expanded this year.

"Some of these increases are caused directly by the decision to go to war," Wolfe said. "Why isn't the federal government helping families pay these higher bills?"

The spirit of giving will take on a different meaning for consumers this year.

Retail sales during the all-important holiday season are expected to grow almost 5% this year and surpass $1 trillion for the first time, according to Bain & Company. But the consulting firm said most of that growth would come from inflation, rather than from consumers buying more products.

And Citi on Wednesday said that increased holiday spending would be mainly driven by higher earners this year. The bank said that's a result of the "K"-shaped economy, a term used to describe a scenario where better-off Americans splash out while others struggle.

Toy prices have climbed more than 9% over the past year and now sit at their highest levels since 2019, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics. Manufacturers have raised prices in response to higher costs for transportation and plastic, according to Chris Byrne, an independent toy industry analyst. The artificial intelligence boom has driven up prices for the chips that power gaming devices and other electronics.

Byrne said that's causing shoppers to focus more on the quality of gifts rather than quantity.

"The toy industry always reflects the culture at large," Byrne said. "It's almost true economically as well. As inflation is rising, people are being a little bit more cautious about what they're buying."

Just 16% of respondents to a Morning Consult survey said they planned to spend more in the 2026 holiday season than they did a year ago. Respondents who were older or had lower earnings were more likely to say their budgets were shrinking, the survey found.

Some are doing away with gifts altogether. A poll from consulting firm TSG and the Electronic Transactions Association found 5% of Americans did not plan to buy any presents in 2026, up three percentage points from last year.

One shopper, Wendy Cooper, set a limit of one gift for each of her three adult children this year. For her teenage daughter, the 56-year-old Atlanta resident has scoured Five Below, the trendy value retailer, for budget-friendly presents.

"It's super stressful," Cooper said. "You have to weigh: Do you guys want lights on and a place to live? Or do you want gifts?"

Across the board, the Bureau of Labor Statistics' consumer price index reaccelerated after the U.S. first struck Iran in late February. Annual inflation rates have begun rising faster than wages in the same period, leaving consumers with less purchasing power.

Even if Americans keep spending for the holidays, economists caution that high prices during this busy shopping period can further drag on their economic outlooks. In the CNBC Voter Poll early this month, about three-fourths of participants rated the economy as poor or not good. Consumer confidence gauges had dropped significantly this year, but have regained ground in the runup to the holiday shopping season.

From her home in Northern Michigan, Brecht is doing everything she can to make Christmas feel special without breaking the bank.

The graduate student has been delivering DoorDash orders in her spare time and listing clothes on resale platforms to save up money for her kids' holiday gifts. Brecht is watching closely for discounts and will look for potential presents at thrift stores in the coming weeks.

If necessary, Brecht said she would turn to credit cards for some holiday purchases. She isn't alone: TSG and ETA's survey found that about one-third of Americans said they expected to go into debt during the holiday season, a roughly 74% increase from 2025.

"I don't really know how we're going to do it," Brecht said. "I'm just praying for good Black Friday deals."

Young adults’ reliance on parental wealth is warping family finances

#107. Microdrama apps chase profits as Hollywood moves into short-form, vertical video

Bogdan Nesvit says his mission is simple: "We are essentially building Disney for the 21st century."

Nesvit is the co-founder of microseries app My Drama, built around the latest, bite-sized form of storytelling. Short, serialized "microdrama" videos are created specifically for mobile devices and have generated billions of views this year. They often feature romantic tropes and typically end in cliffhangers to keep viewers hooked.

The category has exploded at a time when traditional media faces waning interest and YouTube now claims more viewing hours than individual streaming platforms.

The microdrama industry is projected to reach $1.5 billion in revenue in the U.S. in 2026, according to research firm Media Partners Asia. In China, where the format originally gained popularity, microdrama revenue surpassed what was made at the box office in 2024.

"It has the potential to evolve into mainstream entertainment and basically become mobile-first video streaming of the size of Netflix," Nesvit said.

Tech companies and startups such as My Drama currently lead the category in metrics like downloads and time spent on the apps.

TikTok's PineDrama is the most popular microdrama app of 2026 so far with 13.2 million U.S. downloads through September, according to Sensor Tower data.

Startups DramaBox and ReelShort beat out entertainment giants Netflix and Disney in average mobile usage time in the U.S. in the third quarter, according to Sensor Tower.

As they see microdramas win over viewers, legacy media companies, Hollywood executives and actors increasingly want in.

Fox Entertainment was one of the earliest Hollywood investors in microdramas. The entertainment studio acquired an equity stake in Holywater Tech, the company behind My Drama, in October 2025.

Fox agreed to produce at least 200 vertical video titles over two years that would be available on the Holywater app. Thus far, hits include dark academia series "Secret Society: Till Blood Do Us Part" and "Kiss of the Pirate King." Individual series have reached tens of millions of views, according to the company.

Nesvit told CNBC in an interview that partnerships like the one between Fox and Holywater benefit studios by providing the infrastructure to publish their short-form videos and get them in front of an audience.

Beyond distributing the videos, Holywater offers advice for what types of content perform best in a short, mobile-first format, according to Nesvit.

"One of the exciting things that we learned when we started talking to studios in Hollywood around two years ago is that all of them are super hungry for consumer data," Nesvit said. "They really want to see how people are interacting with each second of the content."

Nesvit said the company will provide studios with feedback on everything from the length of a camera shot to what color dress a character should wear.

"Our goal is how we can drive audiences from YouTube, from TikTok, from Instagram to our platform, and also create those kinds of synergies [with] legacy media," he said.

Holywater recently adapted Season 3 of Fox's reality TV hit "Farmer Wants a Wife" into a microseries fit for the platform. The company also plans to create a scripted series featuring 30 three-minute episodes inspired by Fox's "Fear Factor: House of Fear."

Holywater currently relies on a combination of subscription fees, which make up about 80% of its revenue, and money from advertisers. Nesvit said the company is also exploring incorporating brand integrations. The company is already profitable, its co-founders said.

Co-founder Anatolii Kasianov said creating the videos is "very cost efficient," with an estimated expense of between $150,000 and $200,000 for an average 70-minute series.

The company's partnerships with content studios, he said, help to bring down customer acquisition costs.

"You attract organically the audience that loves that [intellectual property]," he said.

While Fox saw collaboration as the way into the format, some longtime Hollywood executives are betting they can skip the partnerships and create microdrama platforms in-house.

Jana Winograde, Susan Rovner and Lloyd Braun, who have collectively held leadership roles at NBCUniversal, Showtime, Warner Bros. Discovery and ABC, have jointly founded a microdrama startup called aTwist.

The company, which officially launched in September, has garnered tens of millions of views as of late November across TikTok, Instagram and YouTube, where content lives in addition to the app.

"One of the specialties that we have is really what it means to be a great storyteller," Rovner told CNBC in an interview. "I was president of Warner Bros. Television. I was there for 22 years. During my time there, we had over 18 series go over 100 episodes, so I definitely think we have an expertise in getting people to care about these shows, care about the characters, invest."

"A lot of our competitors' completion rates are very low, their churn rates are very high," Rovner said. "They can often bring people into the store but they can't keep them."

The average churn rate for the most downloaded microdrama apps is nearly 60% so far in 2026, according to Sensor Tower data. The market intelligence firm notes that the number of users lost in each quarter this year has roughly equaled the number of users gained.

ATwist seeks to keep viewers enticed through high-stakes, compelling characters and unique stories. The app has already ordered second seasons of shows and puts a particular emphasis on talent, according to the co-founders.

Jesse Tyler Ferguson, known for his role as Mitchell on "Modern Family," and his husband, Justin Mikita, a Broadway producer, have collaborated on the musical series "Theater Kids vs. Zombies" for aTwist. It premiered on Oct. 15.

Other series that debuted last month included "Honey, I Think Our House Is Haunted" starring Mario Lopez and "Unboxing the Killer Doll" starring Paris Hilton.

In May, aTwist announced an exclusive partnership with Skydance's linear television channel BET to create horizontal microdramas that air first on BET and later stream in vertical format on aTwist's app.

"That brings a lot of awareness to our brand, a lot of marketing," Winograde said. "But it also gives these linear networks the opportunity to get programming at an incredibly low cost that, from what we're seeing, the audience loves."

Winograde suggested aTwist could strike similar partnerships in the future.

Large streaming companies, which have already faced competition from social media apps and YouTube, are exploring ways to incorporate microdramas inside their existing apps.

NBCUniversal's Peacock launched its own vertical video channel within the app this summer that features both original unscripted series and more traditional vertical dramas. NBCUniversal is also eyeing international development by investing in an Indian microdrama platform, EloElo Group's Story TV, earlier this month.

"At NBCUniversal, we see significant opportunity in mobile-first storytelling and emerging formats like microdramas to engage audiences and meet evolving viewing habits," Deepak Arya, executive vice president for corporate strategy and development at NBCUniversal, said in a press release.

Disney+, Paramount+ and Netflix similarly launched vertical channels embedded in their services this year. Notably, the channels are meant to help viewers discover traditional content and so far haven't featured original, vertical-first series.

On top of those media companies, head of Prime Video and Amazon MGM Studios Mike Hopkins told CNBC's Julia Boorstin in an interview last month that the tech company was interested in producing its own microdramas.

Despite the flurry of investment, Hollywood still faces the challenge of how to translate viewing hours into profit, especially for shorter content.

Leading U.S. microdrama app ReelShort posted a net loss of $12 million in 2025 despite bringing in total revenue of $785 million, according to research compiled by Media Partners Asia, or MPA.

The app, which was founded in 2022 by California-based Crazy Maple Studio, currently reaches about 65 million users every month, according to the company's LinkedIn profile.

However, high marketing and content costs have weighed on the company's bottom line. MPA expects ReelShort to reach profitability for the first time this year, with an estimated $40 million in net profit.

Media Partners Asia CEO Vivek Couto told CNBC that acquiring new users is the largest cost, with ReelShort spending an estimated 55% of its revenue in 2025 on marketing.

Couto also noted high content spend, especially since microdrama apps typically require a high volume of content.

"The content is very hit-driven … it wears out fast," Couto said. "The churn rate for this sector is high, so you have to fill that slate all the time, and you have to win back those users."

Correction: A previous version misstated Holywater Tech's full name in first reference. Co-founder Anatolii Kasianov also said expenses run between $150,000 and $200,000 for an average 70-minute series. A previous version misstated the average series length.

#106. Faisal Islam: Why Trump's new tariffs are less of a threat

There was none of last April's fanfare, the Rose Garden reveal, the gameshow blue board or "Liberation Day" rhetoric surrounding Trump's tariff announcement this week.

Instead there is something rather tentative about this latest exercise with talk of further negotiations.

"We expect to have a broad, even a plurilateral negotiation to talk about excess capacity and how to resolve it," US Trade Representative Jamieson Greer told CNBC.

Around the world the view is that this week's announcement is an attempt to shore up the domestic legal underpinning of these tariffs, more than a fundamental change in the Trump trade agenda.

The tariffs are also not due to come in until January or possibly February, after a period of consultation. There has already been eight months of process and hearings, and the rates announced are upper ceilings, often up for negotiation. It's quite the contrast to the overnight shock meted out to the world last April.

Still, the USA is making the argument that large swathes of the rest of the world, including allies, are harming the US goods trade position by supporting their own industries. This is the basis for the tariffs, in large part replacing the previous tariffs justified on "forced labour" provisions.

The UK, which was unaffected by the new tariff announcements, remains caught by the forced labour provisions, albeit at a rate of 10% extra on global tariffs. There was no move in response to the UK's digital services tax.

It means for many important countries there are now two legal bases upon which the Trump tariffs rest, after the Supreme Court struck down the original emergency basis earlier this year. A group of 25 states has taken the Trump administration to court to strike down the forced labour tariffs.

The tariffs will have to be finalised under a Democrat Congress, which has influence, but not power. The Speaker-designate Hakeem Jeffries said this week that the new House would in its first 100 days "move aggressively toâ¦ repeal the Trump tariffs".

The Chinese rate of 20% is the truce rate from last year's Trump-Xi deal. The truce is due to expire in mid-January. Beijing's response was muted, no retaliation, criticism of industrial overcapacity as a "false issue", and a call to implement the Xi-Trump deal. The leverage over China has weakened, with the US share of its exports dropping to around 10% this year, down from 15%, just two years ago, and 19% in 2018.

US announces new tariffs on host of countries including China

New UK-EU treaty has 10-year termination clause

Faisal Islam: This cost-cutting UK-EU deal doesn't mean lower prices will follow

However, the most interesting spot to focus on is closer to home for Trump. Over half of Canada tariffs are authorised under "Section 338" of the 1930s Smoot-Hawley act. There is already a Senate Bill to repeal them, and it is possible that there would be enough rebel Republicans in the House and Senate to threaten to over-ride any Presidential veto. The incoming Senate Finance Committee chair Ron Wyden last week said Democrats were focussed on "figuring out a way to beat Trump and his veto".

Before that an interim deal is being sought, and Democrat senators such as Chris Coons are appearing on Canadian TV, telling CBC, external: "Frankly, if you can get an interim deal, I'd take it, and then put it to us in the new majority to legislate an even better deal."

The rest of world is watching the machinations in Washington and also looking beyond Donald Trump's tenure.

It pays to look at how the initial Trump trade-quake has reshaped the pattern of world trade already.

The rest of the world has rerouted its trade flows around Donald Trump's tariff wall, proving far more resilient than expected. In cash terms, China's exports so far in 2026 are down about 8% on 2024, but that is more than 25 times made up for by its increased exports to everywhere else.

Canada has seen flat exports to the USA since tariffs, fewer cars and lumber, but pricier flows of energy. Its exports, especially commodities, to the rest of the world have rocketed.

This means that next month's G20 summit at the president's Miami resort is an important moment. In general US allies have not rocked the boat, accepting the lower level of tariffs as part of the new reality. Could the middle powers choose to upset their host in Florida next month?

The bigger question however, is how much the Democrats decide to keep of the America First trade agenda. It will not be all, but nor will it be nothing. Economists now expect some of these tariffs to stay in place for years.

#103. Buyer beware? Nvidia's volatile swings expose risks of single-stock ETFs

Nvidia's latest bout of volatility is highlighting risks tied to single-stock exchange-traded funds.

Among the hardest hit: the GraniteShares 2x Short NVDA Daily ETF (NVD). The fund sank 63% on Monday after Jensen Huang got reinstated as the chip giant's CEO. Meanwhile, the firm's 2x Long NVDA ETF (NVDL) soared nearly 62% on the same day.

ETF Action founding partner Mike Akins joined "ETF Edge" as Nvidia shares soared 31% during Monday's session, wiping a large portion of the losses from the days following Huang's resignation. Shares cooled on Tuesday and Wednesday, however.

"Historically speaking, we do not see these types of large drawdowns followed by huge drives up in a healthy market," Akins said on Monday. "It usually tends to be when you're in a recession or in a bear market territory, which we're not technically in for the broader market, but we are in for this AI trade."

But it may not be time to go cold turkey on single-stock funds.

"[Leveraged ETFs are] definitely a retail trading vehicle…  I think it's OK to have a little fun trying to trade that," Akins added. "I'm certain there are people that are better at trading these than others. But on the whole, timing the market is very, very hard."

GraniteShares founder and CEO Will Rhind also joined "ETF Edge" on Monday as Nvidia single-stock short ETFs overshadowed his firm's new Anthropic launches: The 2x Short Anthropic Daily ETF (ANS) and the 2x Long Anthropic Daily ETF (AIL).

"We thought this was going to be the biggest news event of the day with Anthropic the first leveraged ETFs going live," he said. "I can tell you this is like a sideshow compared to what we're seeing with Nvidia."

Rhind suggests Nvidia's wild swings aren't dampening enthusiasm for single-stock ETFs.

"As long as there's enthusiasm in the underlying name… people will want to trade it and most likely want to trade it with leverage either on the long on the short side," added Rhind.

"Fast Money" tackled the risks tied to ETFs that are tied to single stocks, too.

"If this is somehow part of a portfolio, [then] that is a dangerous game to be playing," trader Guy Adami said on Monday.

Karen Finerman also addressed the strategy's downsides.

"Leverage is always the thing that kills. It's always leverage," said Finerman, co-founder and CEO of Metropolitan Capital Advisors, in the same interview. "You can survive anything but leverage."

As of Wednesday's close, the GraniteShares 2x Short NVDA Daily ETF plunged 55% so far this week while the GraniteShares 2x Long NVDA Daily ETF is up 31%.

#100. Investors flip the switch on the AI trade

#99. Semiconductor chips are the new oil. Here's why

The artificial intelligence boom has pushed semiconductors past oil and gas in global export value for the first time, according to a Deutsche Bank analysis of International Trade Centre data.

Yet the milestone arrives at an awkward moment, just as investors reassess the outlook for artificial intelligence spending and drive down the price of semiconductor stocks. Last week, before Monday's breathtaking reversal, semiconductor stocks suffered their worst weekly loss in a quarter century, with the PHLX Semiconductor Index (SOX) plunging nearly 11% last Friday alone, capping a more than 17% weekly decline.

Nvidia soared more than 31% Monday after the leading maker of artificial intelligence chips had fallen for a seventh straight week, plunging 46% during that stretch. Then it fell again, dropping almost 10% on Tuesday and Wednesday. Advanced Micro Devices tumbled 20% in two days last Thursday and Friday. Micron Technology sank 27% over the seven days ended last Friday.

"For decades we've talked about the petrodollar. Perhaps increasingly we need to think about the chip dollar," Jim Reid, global head of macro and thematic research at Deutsche Bank wrote in a report this month.

Semiconductor exports overtook oil and gas for the first time during the January-March quarter of 2026, reaching about $1.61 trillion, compared with $1.53 trillion for oil and gas, the report said, using four-quarter rolling totals.

The gap widened in the second quarter, when semiconductor exports totaled about $1.60 trillion and oil and gas exports reached about $1.47 trillion. Disruptions to oil supplies through the Strait of Hormuz after the Iran war helped widen the gap. Daily oil flows through the Strait collapsed nearly 77%, to 4.9 million barrels in the second quarter of 2026 from 21.6 million barrels in the fourth quarter of 2025, according to the U.S. Energy Information Administration.

Yet the demand for semiconductors has proven insatiable in the face of soaring data center construction, boosting global trade in the increasingly scarce AI component. Hundreds of billions of dollars of orders for graphics processing units and data centers aim at providing AI computing power, Rothschild & Co. Redburn wrote in a report last Thursday.

"Compute will be the new commodity fueling this transformation on a global scale," Rothschild analyst Cara Thomson said.

The shift toward chips has macroeconomic implications just as the explosion in oil trade did in the 1970s.

Oil-exporting countries historically recycled much of their surpluses into the purchase of U.S. Treasuries and other bonds, helping finance U.S. deficits and underpin demand for other government debt.

Reid at Deutsche Bank said that since semiconductor-surplus countries in Asia are more closely tied to U.S. tech companies, some chip-export earnings could flow back into U.S. technology stocks, startups and data centers, further helping to finance the AI investment cycle.

The changing dynamics in global trade could also tilt the mix of global financial flows, Reid said. "So welcome to the chip-dollar era: potentially more growth, more excitement, more volatility, and structurally higher yields" in corporate and government debt.
